===== Liu2024_NatGenet_PeaGenome-MendelianTraits =====

Nature Genetics | Volume 56 | September 2024 | 1964–1974
1964
nature genetics
Article
https://doi.org/10.1038/s41588-024-01867-8
Reference genome sequence and population 
genomic analysis of peas provide insights 
into the genetic basis of Mendelian and other 
agronomic traits
Na Liu 
  1,7, Xiaolong Lyu 
  2,7, Xueying Zhang1, Guwen Zhang1, Ziqian Zhang2, 
Xueying Guan 
  2, Xiaoyang Chen3, Xiaoming Yang4, Zhijuan Feng1, Qiang Gao2, 
Wanghong Shi2,5, Yayuan Deng2,5, Kuang Sheng2, Jinwen Ou1, Yumeng Zhu2, 
Bin Wang1, Yuanpeng Bu1, Mingfang Zhang 
  2,5 
, Liangsheng Zhang 
  2,6 
, 
Ting Zhao 
  2 
 & Yaming Gong 
  1 
Peas are essential for human nutrition and played a crucial role in the 
discovery of Mendelian laws of inheritance. In this study, we assembled the 
genome of the elite vegetable pea cultivar ‘Zhewan No. 1’ at the chromosome 
level and analyzed resequencing data from 314 accessions, creating a 
comprehensive map of genetic variation in peas. We identified 235 candidate 
loci associated with 57 important agronomic traits through genome-wide 
association studies. Notably, we pinpointed the causal gene haplotypes 
responsible for four Mendelian traits: stem length (Le/le), flower color (A/a), 
cotyledon color (I/i) and seed shape (R/r). Additionally, we discovered the 
genes controlling pod form (Mendelian P/p) and hilum color. Our study also 
involved constructing a gene expression atlas across 22 tissues, highlighting 
key gene modules related to pod and seed development. These findings 
provide valuable pea genomic information and will facilitate the future 
genome-informed improvement of pea crops.
Legumes are essential for global food security and are one of the main 
sources of plant-based protein for both humans and animals world-
wide1. Based on Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO; https://www.
fao.org/faostat/) datasets, pea (Pisum sativum L., 2n = 14) is one of the 
most important and the fourth most highly produced legume globally, 
following soybean, common bean and chickpea. Consumable peas 
can be classified into two types: grain pea (also known as dry pea and 
field pea) and vegetable pea (also known as green peas)2–5. Vegetable 
peas are harvested with immature seeds (vegetable-seed pea) or edible 
pods (vegetable-pod pea) when the pods are fully filled or not, respec-
tively. As a domesticated crop, pea has been cultivated for more than 
10,000 years6–8. The earliest archeological findings of pea can be dated 
to the late Neolithic era in the Mediterranean Basin and the Near East7,9. 
Owing to its extensive reservoir of genetic diversity, pea is adapted to 
diverse environments and culinary customs across Asia, Europe, Africa, 
the Americas and Oceania8,9.

assembled genome was annotated as repetitive sequences (Supple-
mentary Table 5). Long terminal repeats (LTRs) were the most abun-
dant elements, representing 23.14% of the genome size, including 
45.34% Gypsy-like and 11.48% Copia-like LTR elements (Supplementary 
Table 5). The proportion of repeat sequence in PeaZW1 was compa-
rable with those present in PeaZW6 (3.25 Gb)3 and more than that in 
PeaCaméor (2.66 Gb)9.
Genome assessment and comparative genome analysis
We performed remapping of the Illumina NGS reads (221.54 Gb) to 
PeaZW1. The results revealed a high mapping ratio of 99.33% and 
uniform coverage of 99.77% across almost all genomic regions. The 
genome showed a collinearity between the chromosome-level assem-
bly and the previous reported genetic map3,23 (Supplementary Fig. 4). 
The gene annotation assessment by BUSCO (benchmarking universal 
single-copy orthologs) is 99.44%. The overall base accuracy of PeaZW1 
was estimated to be 7.74 × 10−4 (QV score, 39.00; k-mer completeness, 
99.9874%) based on mapped k-mers using software merqury24 (Supple-
mentary Table 3). No traces of bacterial or fungal DNA were detected in 
the assembled genome (Supplementary Fig. 5). Each line of evidence 
indicated that the de novo PeaZW1 assembly is of high quality. The 
genome sequence alignment between PeaZW6 and PeaZW1 reveals 
high collinearity (Supplementary Fig. 6a). We identified 2,932 Mb of 
collinear blocks and 162 Mb of rearranged regions in each genome. 
The rearrangements consisted of 234 Mb (n = 368) inversions, 187 Mb 
(n = 31,576) translocations, and predominantly polymorphic dupli-
cations, which accounted for 552 Mb (n = 123,269) in the reference 
genomes (Supplementary Fig. 6b). A total of 10,862,422 single nucleo-
tide polymorphisms (SNPs) and 1,226,270 Insertions/deletions (InDels) 
were identified between PeaZW6 and PeaZW1 (Supplementary Fig. 6c). 
Genomic variant annotations and functional effect prediction showed 
that 415 genes were highly impacted by these SNPs/InDels (Supple-
mentary Table 6). Enrichment analysis of these genes showed that they 
were enriched significantly in carbohydrate metabolic process, lipid 
transport and localization, response to symbiotic fungus and hydrogen 
peroxide catabolic process (Supplementary Table 7).
Genomic variation maps and population structures of pea
To elucidate the population structures of pea, a panel of 314 accessions 
were selected, including 237 resequenced and 77 published datasets3 
that consisted mainly of wild species (Pisum fulvum and Pisum elatius), 
domesticated species (Pisum abyssinicum) and wild progenitors of 
pea (Supplementary Table 8). We resequenced the 237 peas includ-
ing 74 grain type, 65 Veg-pod, 48 Veg-seed and 50 afila pea accessions 
(Supplementary Table 8). More than 16.38 trillion base pairs (bp) of 
Illumina short reads, with an average depth of 12.88× of PeaZW1 were 
generated (Supplementary Table 8). After alignment against the refer-
ence PeaZW1 genome, a total of 497,546,431 SNPs were identified across 
314 accessions. Among them, 28,912,046 were high-quality SNPs that 
were used for further population genetic analysis, corresponding to 
7.35 high-quality SNPs per kilobase (Supplementary Table 9).
Faststructure25 was used to construct the population structure for 
clusters (K) 1 to 10. The marginal likelihood plateau was determined 
at K = 6 (Supplementary Fig. 7). Based on the phylogenetic analyses, 
we classified the outgroups, which consisted P. fulvum, P. elatius and 
P. abyssinicum (Fig. 2a). We classified the population of P. sativum into 
three groups (P. sativum I, II and III). P. sativum I consisted primarily of 
wild progenitors3, P. sativum II included most grain pea accessions and 
some Veg-pod type pea accessions, and P. sativum III comprised pri-
marily Veg-pod, Veg-seed and afila-type pea accessions (Fig. 2a). Both 
principal component analysis (PCA) and structure analysis yielded this 
similar clustering pattern (Fig. 2b). There are distinct phenotypic differ-
ences between Veg-pod-type peas in P. sativum II and P. sativum III. For 
example, Veg-pod-type peas in P. sativum III generally have no marbling 
pattern on the surface of dry seeds, while more than 30% of Veg-pods 
Peas have been studied as a genetic model since Gregor Mendel 
uncovered the principles of genetics through hybridization assays 
with peas in the nineteenth century6,10–13. Mendel established the laws 
of segregation and independent assortment by studying seven char-
acteristic traits (seed shape, cotyledon color, pod color, flower color, 
stem length, pod form and position of flowers) of pea, serving as the 
beginning and foundation of modern genetics12. To date, the Mendelian 
traits of R/r14, Le/le15–18, I/i19,20 and A/a21 loci have been characterized using 
molecular technologies22, but the genomic basis of many crucial agro-
nomic traits in pea, including the seven Mendelian traits, have not yet 
been fully comprehended. Two pea genomes have been sequenced3,9. 
The improved pea reference genome and the genome diversity of 118 
cultivated and wild pea accessions by Yang et al.3 highlighted genomic 
features and evolutionary characteristics of peas. However, the com-
parative genome evolution and genomic differentiation of vegetable 
and grain peas as well as the genetic basis of many crucial agronomic 
traits in pea have not been explored.
In this study, we generated a chromosome-scale de novo assembly 
of the genome of a vegetable-type elite pea cultivar ‘Zhewan No. 1’ (ZW1) 
using PacBio HiFi combined with Hi-C technology. In total, we used 314 
accessions (237 resequenced and 77 sets of published data), encom-
passing resequencing data from wild relatives, landraces and cultivars 
to resolve the population structures and evolution history of pea. We 
uncovered 235 candidate loci associated with 57 important agronomic 
traits using genome-wide association studies (GWAS). In particular, we 
anchored the gene haplotypes of PsGA3ox1, PsbHLH, PsSGR, PsSBE1, 
PsCLE42 and PsPPO1 associated with the five Mendelian traits Le, A, I, 
R and P and the important agronomic trait of hilum color, respectively.
Results
Genome assembly of the elite vegetable-type pea cultivar ZW1
Vegetable peas are morphologically and physiologically distinct 
from grain peas in the areas of selection and improvement (Fig. 1a). 
Grain pea is harvested after seed maturation and seeds are used for 
food sources. In contrast, vegetable peas are harvested as immature 
seeds (vegetable-seed (Veg-seed) pea) or edible pods (vegetable-pod 
(Veg-pod) pea) (Fig. 1a). Vegetable peas are further divided into con-
sumption types of fresh, canned and frozen peas. As a result, Veg-seed 
peas usually have bigger seeds and pods, and also have more seeds 
per pod compared with grain peas (Student’s t-test, P value < 0.001) 
(Fig. 1b). Besides, the seeds of Veg-seed peas are often wrinkled or pitted 
at maturity, while grain peas are round in seed shape. Veg-pod peas have 
constricted pods, whereas others have inflated pods (Fig. 1b); afila-type 
peas have extra tendrils and no leaflet (Fig. 1a). Pea is an important 
leguminous vegetable worldwide, with China being both the main 
consumer and producer of vegetable peas (Supplementary Fig. 1).
The inbred pea cultivar ‘ZW1’ for genome sequencing is one of the 
most popular and elite vegetable-seed pea cultivars in China charac-
terized by indeterminate growth habit, and wrinkled dry seeds (Fig. 1c 
and Supplementary Fig. 2). Herein, we used PacBio HiFi sequencing 
and chromosome conformation capture sequences (Hi-C) scaffolding 
to construct a high-quality chromosome-level reference assembly of 
ZW1 (named PeaZW1; Fig. 1d). In total, 106.90 Gb of PacBio HiFi reads 
and 304.67 Gb Hi-C reads were generated (Supplementary Tables 1 
and 2). We assembled the PeaZW1 genome of 3.93 Gb with contig N50 
of 4.23 Mb (Supplementary Table 3). Approximately 99.97% of the final 
PeaZW1 assembly was assigned to seven chromosomes, with scaffold 
N50 of 533.44 Mb (Supplementary Fig. 3 and Supplementary Tables 3 
and 4). In total, 43,957 protein-coding genes in the PeaZW1 genome 
were annotated. The average gene length was 5,524 bp, with an aver-
age of 4.21 exons per gene, similar to prediction in PeaZW6 (ref. 3). 
Additionally, noncoding RNAs (ncRNAs) in the PeaZW1 genome were 
further annotated, which include 137 microRNA (miRNAs), 14,875 
ribosomal RNAs (rRNAs), 21,546 transfer RNAs (tRNAs) and 8,568 small 
nuclear RNAs (snRNAs). A total of 3.43 Gb, representing 87.27% of the

in P. sativum II have marbling patterns on the seed surface (Supple-
mentary Fig. 8a). The color of Veg-pod cotyledons in P. sativum II is 
mostly yellow, while almost 40% of Veg-pod cotyledons in P. sativum III 
are green (Supplementary Fig. 8b). The plant height of Veg-pods in 
P. sativum II is generally taller than that in P. sativum III (Supplemen-
tary Fig. 8c). However, in terms of pod length, pod width, dry seed 
length, seed width and seed perimeter, the Veg-pods in P. sativum III 
are larger than those in P. sativum II (Supplementary Fig. 8d–h).
The genome-wide linkage disequilibrium (LD) decay rate of 
the P. sativum population was estimated to be 100 kb (Supplemen-
tary Fig. 9). Among the three groups of P. sativum, P. sativum II was 
related closely to P. sativum III with population divergence (fixation 
statistics (FST) = 0.169) (Fig. 2c). P. sativum III exhibited the lowest 
nucleotide diversity (π = 1.05 × 10−3) and the highest LD decay among the 
P. sativum I, II and III (Supplementary Fig. 9). The genomic regions with 
the largest differentiation between the P. sativum III and P. sativum II 
are located on chromosome (Chr)1, Chr2 and Chr7 (Supplementary 
Fig. 10). Interestingly, the genes in this region were also associated 
with symbiotic fungus and lipid transport (Supplementary Table 10).
Candidate gene loci associated with Mendelian traits
To explore the phenotypic diversity of pea across different groups, 
natural populations of the 237 resequenced accessions were phe-
notyped for two consecutive years (Supplementary Table 11 and 
Supplementary Fig. 11) and 57 important agronomic traits in pea 
were quantified and analyzed by GWAS, including important yield,

Supplementary Table 12 and Source Data Fig. 3). Here, we charac-
terized the candidate genes responsible for six Mendelian traits 
(A, LE, I, R, GP and V) by GWAS (Supplementary Table 13).

The Le gene was found to encode a gibberellin 3-oxidase 
(PsGA3ox1) that is necessary for activation of gibberellins (GAs)16. Using 
a GWAS approach, we found a significant signal (Chr5: 654,559,793) on 
the exon of Pisum05G532700 (PsGA3ox1) associated with stem length 
(Fig. 3b–d). A mutation from G to A resulted in a change from Ala to Thr, 
which is consistent with previous reports16,26. In addition, we observed 
that the mutation on PsGA3ox1 is associated with the length of leaflet, 
stipule and peduncle as well (Supplementary Fig. 12), indicating the 
pleiotropic role of PsGA3ox1.
Mendel noted that flower color often occurs together with 
pigmented leaf axils, indicating the pleiotropic effect caused by a 
single gene locus (A)12. Here, we found that a pleiotropic locus on 
Pisum06G115000 (PsbHLH) encoding a bHLH transcription factor, is 
relevant not only to the flower color and pigmented leaf axil, but also 
to seedling hypocotyl color, stem color, dry seed color and marbling 
pattern on the surface of dry seed (Fig. 3e and Supplementary Fig. 13). 
There was one splice donor site variant (Chr6: 84,341,135) in PsbHLH 
(Fig. 3f,g). These findings are consistent with a previous report of an 
insertion that affects a splice region in an exon21.
The cotyledons with green (i) color were observed by Mendel, 
as opposed to yellow (I) cotyledons. GWAS of cotyledon color iden-
tified a significant locus on Chr2, which harbored the previously 
reported stay-green G (PsSGR) gene (Pisum02G311400)20 (Fig. 3h). 
We found one nonsynonymous variant and one splice region site vari-
ant in the PsSGR (Supplementary Table 13). The mutation from T to 
A (Chr2:490,224,530) resulted in a change from Thr to Ser (Fig. 3i–j), 
which caused a change in the color of the cotyledons.
Furthermore, a significant locus on Chr3 was identified to be asso-
ciated with another Mendelian trait, seed shape (R), which harbored a 
known gene, starch branching enzyme1 (PsSBE1, Pisum03G082600)14 
(Fig. 3k,l), involved in starch biosynthesis. No nonsynonymous variant 
was detected in the coding region. However, two splice region site variants 
and 110 associated SNP variants were detected. Most of these variants are 
located in intron regions (Supplementary Table 13). A 4.0 kb sequence 
deletion in the third intron region was detected in PsSBE1 (Fig. 3m), which 
is longer than the previously reported deletions of 800 bp14 and 9 bp3.
The pod color locus (GP) is one of the remaining Mendelian loci that 
was mapped to a region corresponding to pea Chr3 in previous studies27,28. 
Shirasawa et al.29 found one candidate gene for GP that was predicted to 
be a 3′-exoribonuclease (Psat0s4355g0080) and mapped to a single locus 
on Chr3LG5 at 93.3 cM. In our study, we found that Pisum03G256280 
was homologous with Psat0s4355g0080. One SNP variation from T to C 
(Chr3: 320,905,427) in its promoter was found exclusively in accessions 
displaying yellow pod coloration (Supplementary Fig. 14).
Dissection of genetic loci for seed size and the first flower node 
position traits
In addition to the Mendelian loci, several potential causative genes/
loci were also uncovered for many important agronomic traits in pea. 
The improvement of pod and seed yield is one of the most important 
goals in legume breeding. Many pod and seed yield related loci were 
identified in this study, including the fresh and dry pod size and weight, 
pod wall thickness, seed size and 100-seed weight (Source Data Fig. 3). 
The putative candidate genes of some agronomic traits were identified 
in this study (Supplementary Table 14). Among them, one significant 
signal was associated strongly with seed size (seed diameter, length and 
surface area) on Chr2 by GWAS (Supplementary Fig. 15a–f), which con-
tained a Kinesin-like protein (Pisum02G005300). Kinesin-like proteins 
play a crucial role in regulating microtubule dynamics and previous 
studies have shown that certain kinesin-like proteins are involved in 
the control of cell elongation and seed size30. In this study, we discov-
ered two mutations at a splice region site that might be related to seed 
size of peas (Supplementary Fig. 15g). Additionally, we located a heat 
shock protein 90 (HSP90, Pisum05G126900) on Chr5, which might be 
related to dry seed size (Supplementary Fig. 16). HSP90 involvement 
in both seed filling and the metabolism processes of carbon and nitro-
gen has been reported in maize31 and wheat32. The expression pattern 
showed a specific role in seed development (Supplementary Fig. 16). 
Additionally, the node position of the first flower is associated with the 
harvesting time of pea. Here, we identified an isochorismate synthase 
2 gene (PsICS2, Pisum07G418100) associated with the node position 
of the first flower (Supplementary Fig. 17). ICS2 has been reported to 
play roles in regulating flowering time33,34. In addition, we confirmed 
that the af gene (Pisum02G300400) was located on Chr2 and related 
to the afila mutation (Supplementary Fig. 11f)35.
Dissection of genetic loci for pod form and seed hilum traits
The pod form locus (V/P) is one of the remaining Mendelian loci. 
Veg-pod peas are edible due to the constricted pod with parchment-free 
pod wall, while Veg-seed peas and grain peas have an inflated pod with 
a parchment pod wall (Fig. 4a). V and P loci have been mapped on Chr5 
and Chr1, respectively36,37. In this study, we revealed a significantly asso-
ciated locus on Chr1 (373–393 Mb) by GWAS (Fig. 4b). In addition, an 
F2 population (constricted pod cultivar ZWR35 × inflated pod cultivar 
ZW071) was developed and the bulked segregant analysis (BSA) result 
showed two significant peak signals on Chr1 and Chr5, in which the peak 
on Chr1 was in accordance with the GWAS result (Fig. 4c).
Within the GWAS signal at 378–391 Mb, six genes with nonsynony-
mous variations in the LD block were obtained. By analyzing nonsyn-
onymous SNPs in the candidate genes, we found Pisum01G262600, 
which encodes a CLAVATA3/ESR-related (CLE) protein homologous to 
AtCLE42 and was named PsCLE42 (Fig. 4d,e). CLE is a signaling peptide 
that regulates stem cell identity and cell division38. A deleterious muta-
tion (Chr1:388,149,702) from T to A caused a premature stop codon 
(Fig. 4f). Haplotype analysis showed that the mutation was presented 
predominantly in the Veg-pods group but not in the others (Fig. 4g). 
The RNA sequencing (RNA-seq) data across different tissues of pea 
showed that PsCLE42 exhibited high expression levels during pod

development (Fig. 4h). Quantitative PCR with reverse transcription 
(RT–qPCR) analysis showed that the expression of PsCLE42 was four-
fold higher in the inflated pod cultivar than in the constricted-thin and 
constricted-thick cultivars (Fig. 4i). Thus, PsCLE42 is thought to be the 
causal gene for pod form variation.
The hilum is a specific structure clearly visible on mature seeds 
(Fig. 5a). Here, through GWAS analysis, we found that hilum color 
displayed a strong association signal on Chr1 (454–457 Mb) (Fig. 5a). 
To validate the locus related to hilum, an F2 population (black hilum 
pea accession JP2 × white hilum pea accession Cuizhu) was developed 
and the BSA result showed one significant peak signal on Chr1 (Fig. 5b). 
This signal overlapped with the GWAS signal associated with the gene 
Pisum01G356000 encoding a polyphenol oxidase, homologous to 
PsPPO1 (ref. 39) (Supplementary Fig. 18). According to the screening of 
nonsynonymous mutations in this locus, a deletion (Chr1: 456,785,103) 
in the exon of PsPPO1 resulted in frameshift mutation (Fig. 5c–e). Haplo-
type analysis demonstrated that the deletion was associated with white 
hilum (Fig. 5f). The percentage of nonfunctional PsPPO1 haplotype 
was ascending from P. sativum I to P. sativum III, suggesting that this 
haplotype was fixed in most pea cultivars (Fig. 5g).
Gene modules controlling pod and seed development
We selected 22 different tissues of ZW1 pea plant at various develop-
mental stages to conduct RNA-seq analysis (Fig. 6a and Supplementary 
Table 15). A total of 19 modules were identified using weighted gene 
coexpression network analysis (WGCNA) (16,446 genes) (Fig. 6b and 
Supplementary Fig. 19). Three modules, totaling 800 genes, displayed 
a relatively higher correlation with pod development, which were func-
tionally associated mainly with secondary cell wall biosynthesis and 
lignin catabolic process (Fig. 6c and Supplementary Tables 16 and 17).

When exploring genes in the Lightcyan1 module, we found a regulatory 
network that may regulate secondary cell wall biosynthesis, includ-
ing homologous genes of MYB58, MYB63, 4CL1, IRX9, CCoAOMT1, 
CYP98A3, PGSIP3 and IRX6 (ref. 40) (Supplementary Fig. 19). Two mod-
ules involving 5,499 genes showed a relatively higher correlation with 
seed development (Fig. 6c and Supplementary Table 16). Gene ontology 
term enrichment analyses of these genes highlighted key biological 
processes in seed development, such as soluble sugar, starch, lipid 
and protein metabolism (Supplementary Table 17). Besides, we also 
identified one gene module involving 117 genes, expressed specifically 
in root nodule (Fig. 6c and Supplementary Table 16). These specifically 
expressed genes in root nodules may provide valuable reference for 
future research on the mechanism underlying the symbiotic nitrogen 
fixation of peas. The temporal transcriptome was then analyzed using 
k-means clustering across five seed and pod developmental stages 
(5 days after pollination (DAP) to 25 DAP). These modules genes exhib-
ited six expression patterns in both seed and pod development (Fig. 6d 
and Supplementary Table 18). Genes with the same expression pat-
tern may participate in the same biological process, which provides 
important information for the subsequent study of the regulatory 
network of pea seed and pod development. We also identified 284 
genes close to GWAS loci that were expressed specifically during seed 
development (Fig. 6e). In addition, we found that genes related to the 
metabolism of soluble sugar, starch, lipid and protein metabolism were 
activated more significantly in ZW1 compared with ZW6 (ref. 5) (Fig. 6f 
and Supplementary Table 19), which further illustrates the metabolic 
differences between these two pea cultivars.
Discussion
Apart from serving as the model plant used by Mendel to discover the 
modern genetic laws in nineteenth century41, pea represents an ecof-
riendly and economically important pulse crop cultivated worldwide. 
The first grain pea genome was released by Kreplak et al.9 in 2019, and 
some seed storage protein (SSP) loci were identified by homology 
alignment and gene expression analysis. In 2022, Yang et al.3 reported 
another pea genome and uncovered two known Mendel’s genes related 
to stem length (Le/le) and seed shape (R/r), and a quantitative trait locus 
for pod form using a F2 population. Herein, we assembled a high-quality 
reference genome of elite vegetable pea ZW1 and resequenced 237 
accessions. Combining with previous reported resequencing data of 
77 accessions3, we constructed genetic variation maps of 314 peas and 
identified 235 candidate loci/genes associated with 57 agronomic traits 
using GWAS. We found that Veg-pod peas are distributed in P. sativum II 
and III (Fig. 2), indicating that Veg-pod peas underwent a complex evo-
lutionary history or possibly some genes determining its variability.
In this study, we not only identified the previously reported four 
Mendelian gene variants at the R, Le, I and A loci, but also found new 
haplotypes of these Mendelian genes (Supplementary Table 13). For 
the R locus, we did not find the previously reported 0.8 kb sequence 
presence/absence variant (PAV), but identified one 4.0 kb PAV and 112 
SNP variants with most in the intron regions of the PsSBE1 gene that 
were highly associated with seed shape (Supplementary Table 13), 
indicating several variations of this gene in natural pea populations. 
Further, we revealed the remaining V/P and GP Mendel’s loci for pod 
form and pod color traits. For GP, the reported nonsynonymous vari-
ant (G to T) in the first coding sequence of Psat0s4355g0080 was also 
found in our 13 resequencing accessions (SNP-Chr3:320,903,853 inside 
Pisum03G256280). However, both yellow (2 accessions) and green 
(11 accessions) pod color could be observed in these 13 accessions 
with the same mutated allele, while 131 accessions with the reference 
Pisum03G256280 allele exhibited green pod color. Collectively, we 
speculated that there are different variations on Psat0s4355g0080 in 
different varieties, leading to the pod color change. For the pod form 
trait, according to Mendel’s original notes, pod form is controlled by a 
single gene12. This trait is controlled by two loci: V and P (ref. 42). V and 
P loci have been mapped to Chr5 and Chr1. It is not clear which locus 
had been segregating in Mendel’s test population. Yang et al.3 identi-
fied one possible candidate gene (Psat05G0794700) associated with 
pod form on Chr5 using an F2 population. In our study, we reported the 
PsCLE42 on Chr1 as the candidate gene for the P locus by using GWAS, 
BSA and transcriptome approaches. The PsPPO1 gene encoding a 
polyphenol oxidase was shown to be the causal gene for seed hilum 
color. As white seed hilum color is a favorite trait for canned or frozen 
vegetable pea processing43, the finding of this hilum color gene will 
facilitate the marker-assisted breeding of pea cultivars for processing 
use. Spatiotemporal transcriptomic datasets also provide a global 
gene expression atlas and will facilitate the functional genomics study 
of pea. Collectively, we accomplished the first high-quality reference 
genome of vegetable pea and provided a global landscape of genomic 
variations and candidate gene loci associated with Mendelian traits 
and beyond. The genome and transcriptome resources generated in 
this study will enable genomic selection and improvement of fresh and 
processed vegetable peas.
Online content
Any methods, additional references, Nature Portfolio reporting sum-
maries, source data, extended data, supplementary information, 
acknowledgements, peer review information; details of author contri-
butions and competing interests; and statements of data and code avail-
ability are available at https://doi.org/10.1038/s41588-024-01867-8.

Coupling metabolomics and exome 
sequencing reveals graded effects of rare 
damaging heterozygous variants on gene 
function and human traits

Genetic studies of the metabolome can uncover enzymatic and transport 
processes shaping human metabolism. Using rare variant aggregation testing 
based on whole-exome sequencing data to detect genes associated with 
levels of 1,294 plasma and 1,396 urine metabolites, we discovered 235 gene–
metabolite associations, many previously unreported. Complementary 
approaches (genetic, computational (in silico gene knockouts in whole-body 
models of human metabolism) and one experimental proof of principle) 
provided orthogonal evidence that studies of rare, damaging variants in the 
heterozygous state permit inferences concordant with those from inborn 
errors of metabolism. Allelic series of functional variants in transporters 
responsible for transcellular sulfate reabsorption (SLC13A1, SLC26A1) 
exhibited graded effects on plasma sulfate and human height and pinpointed 
alleles associated with increased odds of diverse musculoskeletal traits 
and diseases in the population. This integrative approach can identify new 
players in incompletely characterized human metabolic reactions and reveal 
metabolic readouts informative of human traits and diseases.
A complex interplay of thousands of enzymes and transport proteins 
is involved in maintaining physiological levels of intermediates and 
end products of metabolism. Disturbances of their function can result 
in severe diseases, such as those caused by inborn errors of metabo-
lism (IEMs), or predispose to common metabolic diseases such as type 
2 diabetes or gout. While the study of rare, early-onset, autosomal 
recessive IEMs has uncovered many metabolite-related genes, such 
studies are limited by the very low number of persons homozygous for 
the causative variants. Conversely, genome-wide association studies 
(GWASs) in large populations have revealed thousands of common 
genetic variants associated with altered metabolite levels1–13, but these 
variants’ functional effects are often unknown, and their modest effect 
sizes limit their direct clinical impact.
Gene-based aggregation testing of rare, putatively damaging 
variants in population studies can address this challenge. Previously, 
such studies have focused almost exclusively on the circulating

linkage permits the systematic study of the aggregated and individual 
effects of rare, damaging, metabolite-associated variants on a wide 
variety of traits and diseases.
Here, we set out to perform gene-based rare variant aggregation 
testing to discover genes associated with metabolite levels and to 
characterize their genetic architecture with respect to the identified 
variants and across plasma and urine. We validate identified genes and 
variants and the range of their effects through complementary genetic 
approaches, with a new computational method based on WBMs22,23 
and through proof-of-principle experimental studies, and identify 
traits and diseases for which these metabolites represent molecular 
readouts.
Results
As summarized in Fig. 1, rare, putatively damaging variants were 
identified in 16,525 genes based on WES data from 4,737 GCKD study 
participants (mean age of 60 years, 40% women; Supplementary 
Table 1). Metabolites were determined by nontargeted mass spec-
trometry and covered a wide variety of superpathways (Metabolon 
HD4 platform; Supplementary Table 2). Exome-wide burden tests 
for the association between each gene and the levels of each of 1,294 
plasma and 1,396 urine metabolites (781 overlapping) were carried 
out using two complementary ‘masks’ that differed in the selection 
of qualifying variants (QVs; Methods) for gene-based aggregation. 
While the ‘LoF_mis’ mask contained a median of eight QVs per gene 
predicted to be either high-confidence loss-of-function (LoF) vari-
ants or deleterious missense or in-frame nonsynonymous variants, 
the ‘HI_mis’ mask contained a median of 16 QVs per gene predicted 
metabolome14–20. We have shown recently that GWASs of paired plasma 
and urine metabolomes do not only reveal many more associations 
but also enable specific insights into renal metabolite handling2. We 
therefore aimed to perform gene-based testing of the aggregate effect 
of rare variants on the levels of 1,294 plasma and 1,396 urine metabo-
lites quantified from 4,737 participants in the German Chronic Kidney 
Disease (GCKD) study with whole-exome sequencing (WES) data to 
identify metabolism-related genes and to understand whether the 
underlying rare, almost exclusively heterozygous variants permit 
inferences complementary to the ones obtained from the study of IEMs.
Patients with IEMs typically show severe symptoms that originate 
from accumulation or depletion of metabolites, while heterozygous 
carriers of the causative variants often show milder changes of the same 
or related metabolic phenotypes21. We hypothesized that sex-specific 
analysis of metabolite-associated, X chromosomal genes as well as 
knowledge-based, computational modeling based on sex-specific 
organ-resolved whole-body models (WBMs22; Methods) of human 
metabolism can inform on whether heterozygous damaging vari-
ants capture the metabolic effects of their unobserved homozygous 
counterparts. WBMs enable the investigation of homozygous gene 
defects through deterministic in silico knockout modeling. The result-
ing virtual IEMs reflect observed IEMs22–25. We further hypothesized 
that metabolite-associated rare variants identified in the GCKD study 
would show associations with related human traits and diseases in 
very large population studies and that the genetic effects would be 
proportional to their effects on metabolite levels if the implicated 
metabolites are molecular readouts of disease-relevant processes.

as high-impact consequence (transcript ablation or amplification, 
splice acceptor or donor, stop-gain, frameshift, start or stop lost) or 
as deleterious missense variants using additional prediction scores 
(Methods). Both masks assume a LoF mechanism but account for dif-
ferent genetic architectures.
Discovery of 192 significant gene–metabolite associations
We identified 192 significant gene–metabolite pairs across both plasma 
(P value < 5.04 × 10−9) and urine (P value < 4.46 × 10−9), where 43 asso-
ciations were detected in both (192 + 43 associations overall; Fig. 2a 
and Supplementary Table 3). These involved 73 unique genes and 179 
metabolites, with a comparable number of genes and metabolites iden-
tified in plasma and urine. There were 22 and 17 genes with significant 
associations exclusively in plasma and in urine, respectively. While 
the majority of associations was detected with both masks, the more 
inclusive ‘HI_mis’ mask yielded more mask-specific associations than 
the ‘LoF_mis’ mask (Fig. 2b). Amino acids and lipids were the dominating 
pathways among the associated metabolites (Supplementary Fig. 1). 
The higher proportion of implicated lipids in plasma than in urine is 
consistent with the absence of glomerular filtration of many lipids 
(Fig. 2b). Associations detected in both plasma and urine generally 
affected the levels of the implicated metabolite in the same direction 
(Fig. 2a). Sensitivity analyses evaluating additional masks and methods 
for aggregation testing (LoF only, sequence kernel association test 
(SKAT) and SKAT- optimal unified test (SKAT-O)) as well as sex-stratified 
and kidney function-stratified analyses supported the robustness of 
the main findings (Supplementary Results, Extended Data Figs. 1–3 
and Supplementary Tables 4 and 5).
Previous independent studies of associations between 
sequencing-based rare variants and metabolite levels obtained using 
comparable technology have focused on plasma and serum14,15,19,20. 
Comparison of the 128 discovered gene–plasma metabolite asso-
ciations in this study with previous studies14,15,19,20 showed that 69% 
(88 of 128) were not reported previously, although 93% (82 of 88) of 
the new findings involved metabolites analyzed before (Supplemen-
tary Table 6; detailed description in the Supplementary Methods 
and the Supplementary Results).
The 73 unique metabolite-associated genes were strongly over-
represented among genes known to be causative for IEMs (odds 
ratio = 10.6, P value = 1.9 × 10−14; Supplementary Methods), with 28 
(38%) of them currently known to harbor causative mutations (Sup-
plementary Table 6). The QVs detected in our study of middle-aged 
and older adults were almost exclusively observed in the heterozygous 
state (Supplementary Data 1). Detailed annotation of QVs in the two 
masks (Supplementary Table 7) showed that 63 unique QVs in 15 genes 
and 73 unique QVs in 17 genes were listed in ClinVar as ‘pathogenic’ 
or ‘pathogenic or likely pathogenic’ for a corresponding monogenic 
disease. These observations support the notion that gene-based 
aggregation of rare, heterozygous, putatively damaging variants 
effectively identifies gene–metabolite relationships implicated in 
human diseases.
Validation through independent, complementary approaches
Independent replication of our findings is complicated by differences 
in QVs, metabolite quantification methods and different analytical 
choices across studies. We therefore validated our findings using four 
complementary approaches: first, the large UKB permitted analysis 
of the same rare QVs using the same analytical choices (Methods), 
as in our study for two overlapping metabolites, and showed very 
similar effect sizes for gene–metabolite associations (Fig. 3a). Second, 
the UKB proteomics data26 contain information on circulating levels 
of the encoded proteins of 17 genes implicated in our study. Burden 
tests aggregating protein-truncating and rare damaging variants 
revealed associations with lower levels of 15 of these proteins (in cis, 
P value < 1 × 10−5; Fig. 3b)27, potentially explained by nonsense-mediated 
decay. Third, comparison of our findings to those from a previous 
study of the plasma metabolome15 showed highly correlated effect 
sizes with those from our study, both on the variant level and the 
aggregated level (Spearman correlation coefficient > 0.8; Fig. 3c,d 
and Supplementary Table 8).
Lastly, we performed a proof-of-concept experimental valida-
tion study for an implicated gene–metabolite relationship. The B0AT1 
transporter, encoded by SLC6A19, is responsible for the uptake of 
neutral amino acids across the apical membrane of intestinal and 
kidney epithelial cells28. In addition to associations with the levels of 
the known substrates asparagine, histidine and tryptophan, we also 
detected associations with methionine sulfone, not yet reported as 
a substrate. Transport studies in CHO cells overexpressing human 
SLC6A19 and its co-chaperone collectrin (CLTRN) in comparison to 
the control indeed confirmed methionine sulfone to be a substrate of 
the transporter in vitro, in a similar concentration range as its known 
substrate isoleucine (Fig. 4a and the Methods). Specificity was shown 
by complete inhibition of transport activity upon application of the 
SLC6A19 inhibitor cinromide29 (Fig. 4b). Together, these four com-
plementary lines of evidence all support the validity of the detected 
associations.
Prioritization and characteristics of driver variants
We next performed a forward selection procedure15 to assess the con-
tribution of individual QVs to their gene-based association signals 
(Methods). Plots that visualize the association P value based on the 
successive aggregation of the most influential QVs (Supplementary 
Data 2) revealed noteworthy differences across genes and metabolites, 
with examples detailed in the Supplementary Results.
The inclusion of effectively neutral variants among the QVs may 
dilute their joint signal. We thus prioritized the variants with the 
strongest individual contributions that resulted in the lowest pos-
sible association P value when aggregated for burden testing15 as 
‘driver variants’ (Methods). For each significant association signal, 
we identified at least two and up to 48 driver variants (median of 
13; Supplementary Data 2 and Supplementary Tables 3 and 7). The 
proteins encoded by the vast majority of identified genes are directly 
involved in the generation, turnover or transport of the associated 
Fig. 2 | Overview of the 192 identified gene–metabolite associations

detected in both plasma and urine is dominated by amino acids. Part. charact.,

lost, frameshift and stop-gain variants to variants predicted to affect 
splicing (Extended Data Fig. 4b). The median effect of drivers also 
increased with lower minor allele count and differed substantially 
from the one of nondrivers in each minor allele count bin (Extended 
Data Fig. 4c).

Fig. 3 | Independent validation of findings using orthogonal approaches. 
a, Gene-based testing of significantly associated, available plasma metabolites 
among ≥261,661 UKB participants (y axis) using the same mask and only including 
QVs available in both the GCKD study and the UKB provided very similar effect 
sizes in the two studies (x axis). Bars represent corresponding standard errors 
(SE), symbol color reflects the −log10 (P value), and the size depicts the cumulative 
minor allele frequency of all QVs within a gene. In the UKB, gene-based burden 
tests were performed as implemented in REGENIE (Methods). cMAF, cumulative 
MAF. b, Plasma levels of the proteins encoded by 17 of the 73 significant, 
metabolite-associated genes were measured in the UKB (N ≥ 44,108)27. Among 
the available gene-level summary statistics27, 15 genes showed cis associations 
with plasma protein levels with an association P value < 1 × 10−5 based on a 
‘ptvraredmg’ mask, which is similar to the masks used in the GCKD study. Genes 
are shown on the y axis. Effect sizes and the corresponding standard errors of the 
cis associations with plasma protein levels based on these summary statistics27 
are displayed on the x axis. Symbol color reflects the −log10 (P value). For all 
cis associations, the direction of effect sizes was negative, consistent with LoF 
variants resulting in lower plasma protein levels. c, Single-variant effect sizes on 
levels of a given plasma metabolite in the GCKD study (x axis) were very similar 
to those in the INTERVAL study (Bomba et al.15) (y axis) for all QVs involved in 
significant gene–metabolite associations in the GCKD study that were also 
available in the INTERVAL study and showed an association P value < 0.1 in both 
studies. Horizontal bars indicate standard errors of effect sizes in the GCKD 
study (not available for the INTERVAL summary statistics). The depicted 200 
associations involved 35 unique genes and 75 unique metabolites. Symbol 
color indicates the INTERVAL association P value. Gray lines represent identity 
(dotted) and the linear regression line (dashed). The strong correlation of effect 
sizes supports reproducibility. d, Summary statistics for 89 of 128 plasma gene–
metabolite associations were available in the INTERVAL study (Bomba et al.). 
Effect sizes of gene–metabolite associations on the aggregated level in the GCKD 
study (x axis) were very similar to those in the INTERVAL study (y axis; Methods 
and Supplementary Table 8), despite differences in masks and aggregation unit. 
Horizontal error bars indicate the standard errors of effect sizes in the GCKD 
study. Standard errors were not available in the summary statistics from Bomba 
et al. Symbol shape indicates the corresponding mask used by Bomba et al.  
(LOF, high-confidence LoF variants; MLOF, LoF and missense variants combined; 
CODING, all rare exonic variants, splice sites and variants residing in untranslated 
regions). Color reflects the association P value in the INTERVAL study. Gray lines 
represent the identity (dotted) and the linear regression line (dashed).

Heterozygous variants inform about dose–response effects
The identification of known IEM-causing variants such as in CTH, PAH, 
SLC6A19 and SLC7A9 (Supplementary Table 7) in the heterozygous 
state supports the notion that heterozygous QVs are functional alleles 
that lead to more extreme metabolic changes when present homozy-
gously. For three genes with a homozygous QV present in more than one 
individual in our study, homozygous individuals tended to have more 
extreme metabolite levels than heterozygous ones (Extended Data 
Fig. 6), supporting a dose–response effect. Moreover, we had previ-
ously confirmed experimentally that heterozygous sulfate-associated 
QVs in SLC26A1 detected by aggregate variant testing are indeed LoF 
alleles and that the encoded protein is an important player in human 
sulfate homeostasis30. However, experimental studies of each of the 
2,077 QVs and 73 genes detected here are infeasible, and IEMs are so rare 
that no homozygous person for a given gene may have been observed 
yet. We therefore used three orthogonal approaches: examination of 
hemizygosity, in silico knockout modeling and investigation of variants 
prioritized through allelic series, to evaluate whether the observed 
metabolite-associated heterozygous variants captured similar infor-
mation about a gene’s function as might be derived from homozygous 
damaging variants in the respective gene.
X chromosomal genes as a readout of variant homozygosity
Genes in the non-pseudo-autosomal region of the X chromosome offer 
an opportunity to study differences between heterozygous women 
and effectively homozygous (that is, hemizygous) men. We therefore 
investigated sex differences for the two X chromosomal genes identi-
fied in our screen, TMLHE and RGN (Supplementary Table 10).
Indeed, male carriers of QVs in TMLHE showed clearly higher urine 
levels of N6,N6,N6-trimethyllysine, the substrate of the encoded enzyme 
trimethyllysine dioxygenase, than female carriers as well as markedly 
lower levels of its product hydroxy-N6,N6,N6-trimethyllysine, especially 
when focusing on driver variants (Fig. 5 and Supplementary Table 10). 
In plasma, male QV carriers showed 1.15 s.d. lower levels of plasma 
hydroxy-N6,N6,N6-trimethyllysine than noncarriers (P value = 6 × 10−44), 
whereas female QV carriers only showed 0.45 s.d. lower metabolite 
levels than noncarriers (P value = 3 × 10−4). A similar tendency was 
observed for RGN and urine levels of the unnamed metabolite X-23436. 
Levels were higher among both male and female carriers (Supplemen-
tary Table 10), suggesting that X-23436 is a metabolite upstream of the 
reaction catalyzed by the encoded regucalcin. Data from the GTEx 
Project31 show no sex differences in gene expression across tissues. 
Hence, sex-differential effects on metabolite levels likely represent a 
dose–response effect resulting from heterozygosity versus hemizy-
gosity of the involved QVs.
Virtual IEMs mirror the effects of heterozygous variants
We next investigated the implicated genes’ LoF by generating virtual 
IEMs for 24 genes that covered 60 gene–metabolite pairs via in silico 
knockout modeling (Methods and Extended Data Fig. 7). We compared 
the maximal secretion flux of the implicated metabolite into blood 
and/or urine between the wild-type WBM and the gene-knockout 
WBM. Initially, the direction of the observed gene–metabolite asso-
ciations was correctly predicted by virtual IEMs with an accuracy of 
73.3% in the male WBM and 76.7% in the female WBM, which is sig-
nificantly better than chance (Fisher’s exact test, P value = 3.3 × 10−3 
(male), P value = 1.5 × 10−4 (female); Supplementary Table 11). After 
model curation informed by the observed gene–metabolite asso-
ciations, which included the addition of metabolites (for exam-
ple, 8-methoxykynurenate) and pathways as well as alteration of

Top, plots represent covariate-adjusted urine levels of N6,N6,N6-trimethyllysine

female (right) noncarriers and carriers of QVs in TMLHE based on the HI_mis

Article
https://doi.org/10.1038/s41588-024-01965-7
highlight opportunities to further improve WBMs by curation of the 
underlying knowledge base.
Personalized WBMs capture observed metabolic changes
Virtual IEMs as described above only allow for qualitative prediction. 
To additionally study an equivalent to observed effect sizes, we intro-
duced a second modeling strategy (Extended Data Fig. 7) as proof 
of principle, focusing on the gene KYNU. We successfully generated 
569 microbiome-personalized32 WBMs (Methods) and calculated the 
effect size of in silico KYNU knockout on metabolite excretion into urine 
against the natural variation induced by the personalized microbiomes 
(Supplementary Table 13). Eighteen of 257 metabolites had a modeling 
P value < 0.05/257, implicating them as potential biomarkers of the cor-
responding IEM kynureninase deficiency (Supplementary Table 14). The 
in silico effects of these 18 biomarkers, mostly belonging to tryptophan 
metabolism and the nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide (NAD)+ de novo 
synthesis pathway, were significantly correlated with their observed 
counterparts (Supplementary Fig. 2a). Whereas two of the three metab-
olites with particularly large effects in both in silico modeling and the 
GCKD study, xanthurenate and 3-hydroxykynurenine, are known bio-
markers of kynureninase deficiency33, 8-methoxykynurenate was not. 
We therefore measured absolute levels of these metabolites in urine 
samples from a homozygous patient with kynureninase deficiency and 
her parents34 (Supplementary Methods) and confirmed that, in addi-
tion to xanthurenate and 3-hydroxykynurenine, 8-methoxykynurenate 
also constituted a biomarker of this IEM (Fig. 6a and Extended Data 
Fig. 8), consistent with the association statistics from aggregate tests 
of heterozygous variants from the GCKD study. A similar observation 
was made with regard to the gene PAH (Fig. 6b, Supplementary Fig. 2b 
and Supplementary Results). Thus, in silico knockout modeling of two 
proof-of-principle examples faithfully captured metabolic changes 
observed for heterozygous variants detected in population studies 
and for the corresponding recessively inherited IEMs.
Metabolites represent intermediate readouts of human traits
Allelic series describe a dose–response relationship, in which increas-
ingly deleterious mutations in a gene result in increasingly larger effects 
on a trait or a disease. We hypothesized that genetic effects on metabo-
lite levels should manifest as allelic series if the metabolite represents 
a molecular readout of an underlying (patho-)physiological process. 
As proof of principle, we investigated plasma sulfate because of solid 
evidence for causal gene–metabolite relationships: first, QVs in SLC13A1 
showed a significant aggregate effect on lower plasma sulfate levels 
(P value = 3 × 10−18, lowest possible P value = 2 × 10−25). The observed 
association is well supported by experimental studies establishing 
that the encoded Na+–sulfate cotransporter NaS1 (SLC13A1) reabsorbs 
filtered sulfate at the apical membrane of kidney tubular epithelial 
cells35. Second, we had previously confirmed experimentally that 
plasma sulfate-associated QVs in SLC26A1 are LoF alleles that lead to 
reduced sulfate transport30, consistent with the aggregate effect of 
driver variants in SLC26A1 reaching a P value of 2 × 10−11 for association 
with plasma sulfate (Extended Data Fig. 9). The encoded sulfate trans-
porter SAT1 localizes to basolateral membranes of tubular epithelial 
cells and works in series with NaS1 to mediate transcellular sulfate 
reabsorption (Fig. 7a)36,37.
Based on a growth retardation phenotype in Slc13a1-knockout 
mice38 and an association between SLC13A1 and lower sitting height in 
the UKB (P value = 3 × 10−8; Supplementary Tables 15 and 39), we investi-
gated relations of six functional driver QVs in SLC13A1 and SLC26A1 with 
anthropometric measurements in the UKB (Methods). Supplementary 
Table 16 contains traits with which at least two QVs showed nominally 
significant associations (P value < 0.05). The genetic effect sizes on 
plasma sulfate levels in the GCKD study and both sitting and standing 
heights in the UKB were correlated (Pearson correlation coefficients 
of 0.57 and 0.70, respectively; Fig. 7b). These observations support a 
causal relationship between transcellular sulfate reabsorption and 
human height and designate plasma sulfate as an intermediate readout. 
Additionally, we observed significantly lower standing height among 
carriers of driver variants in SLC13A1 and SLC26A1 than among non-
carriers in a subsample of the GCKD study (N = 3,239) with measured 
height. The aggregated effect size of driver variants in SLC13A1 was 
−0.54 (corresponding to −5.17 cm when height was not inverse normal 
transformed, P value = 1.6 × 10−3; Supplementary Fig. 3a). For SLC26A1, 
we obtained even a stronger effect size of −0.73 (corresponding to 
−6.68 cm, P value = 1.7 × 10−6; Supplementary Fig. 3b).
The first patient homozygous for a LoF stop-gain mutation in 
SLC13A1, p.Arg12*, has just been described39. Aside from sitting height 
>2 s.d. below the normal range, the patient featured multiple skeletal 
abnormalities. Experimental transport studies40 as well as the patient’s 
fractional sulfate excretion of almost 100%39 establish this variant as 
a complete LoF, resulting in renal sulfate wasting. In this study, we 
found that, compared with noncarriers of p.Arg12*, heterozygous car-
riers showed 0.95 s.d. lower plasma sulfate levels (GCKD, 22 carriers, P 
value = 9.9 × 10−10) and 0.08 s.d. lower sitting height (UKB, 2,480 carri-
ers, P value = 2.2 × 10−7). Plasma sulfate measurements from heterozy-
gous carriers therefore are indicative of more extreme phenotypic 
changes in homozygous carriers.
Variants altering sulfate uptake and musculoskeletal traits
Rare LoF variants in SLC13A1 and SLC26A1 have been linked to indi-
vidual musculoskeletal phenotypes through IEMs and GWASs30,41–43. 
We further investigated the association between the same six func-
tional, sulfate-associated QVs in SLC13A1 and SLC26A1 and muscu-
loskeletal disorders, fractures and injuries in the UKB, for which at 
least two carriers with and without disease were present (Methods). 
There were 116 nominally significant (P value < 0.05) associations with 
clinical traits and diseases, 113 of which were associated with increased 
odds of disease (Fig. 7c). For instance, the odds of various fractures 
ranged up to 30.7 (closed fracture of the neck, P value = 2.1 × 10−8, 
NaS1 p.Trp48*; Supplementary Table 17). While the increased odds

whiskers end at the last observed value within 1.5 × (interquartile range) of the 
box. Middle, distribution of the ln-transformed secretion flux of the metabolite 
in mmol per day into urine (y axis) from minimum-norm quadratic programming 
(QP) simulations based on 569 and 567 microbiome-personalized WBMs  
without and with simulated knockout of KYNU and PAH, respectively (x axis).  
a, Right, multiple-reaction monitoring (m/z 220.0 → 174.1) chromatograms of the 
diluted urine of a child with a homozygous loss of KYNU function34 (patient), the 
heterozygous mother and the healthy father (maternal uniparental isodisomy). 
The signal at 12.5 min representing 8-methoxykynurenate is strongly enhanced 
in the patient sample. Chromatograms are normalized to urine creatinine 
concentrations; y axes are normalized to the intensity of the signal in the 
patient’s chromatograms. b, Right, UV–visible chromatograms (570 nm) of 
amino acids (post-column derivatization with ninhydrin) in serum samples of 
a child with a homozygous loss of PAH function (c.1199+1G>C), the compound 
heterozygous father who additionally carries a mild mutation (c.1180G>C) 
and the heterozygous mother. The signal at 85.5 min represents phenylalanine 
(Phe). The signal at 106 min is the internal standard (IS). The reference range 
for phenylalanine concentrations in children is 38–137 µmol l−1 and in adults is 
26–91 µmol l−1.

218
nature genetics
Article
https://doi.org/10.1038/s41588-024-02037-6
Telomere-to-telomere sheep genome  
assembly identifies variants associated with 
wool fineness
 
Ling-Yun Luo 
  1,8, Hui Wu 
  1,8, Li-Ming Zhao 
  2,8, Ya-Hui Zhang1, Jia-Hui Huang1, 
Qiu-Yue Liu3, Hai-Tao Wang3, Dong-Xin Mo1, He-Hua EEr4, Lian-Quan Zhang5, 
Hai-Liang Chen6, Shan-Gang Jia 
  7 
, Wei-Min Wang 
  2 
 & Meng-Hua Li 
  1 
Ongoing efforts to improve sheep reference genome assemblies still 
leave many gaps and incomplete regions, resulting in a few common 
failures and errors in genomic studies. Here, we report a 2.85-Gb gap-free 
telomere-to-telomere genome of a ram (T2T-sheep1.0), including all 
autosomes and the X and Y chromosomes. This genome adds 220.05 Mb 
of previously unresolved regions and 754 new genes to the most updated 
reference assembly ARS-UI_Ramb_v3.0; it contains four types of repeat 
units (SatI, SatII, SatIII and CenY) in centromeric regions. T2T-sheep1.0 has 
a base accuracy of more than 99.999%, corrects several structural errors in 
previous reference assemblies and improves structural variant detection in 
repetitive sequences. Alignment of whole-genome short-read sequences of 
global domestic and wild sheep against T2T-sheep1.0 identifies 2,664,979 
new single-nucleotide polymorphisms in previously unresolved regions, 
which improves the population genetic analyses and detection of selective 
signals for domestication (for example, ABCC4) and wool fineness (for 
example, FOXQ1).
A reference genome assembly of sheep (Ovis aries), one of the first 
domesticated livestock species, is essential to explore their evolu-
tionary history, migration, genetic diversity and causative variants 
underlying specialized traits1–5. As many as 58 sheep assemblies in 
the NCBI database, such as Oar_v4.0 (ref. 6), Oar_rambouillet_v1.0 and 
ARS-UI_Ramb_v2.0 (ref. 7), suffer from issues such as many gaps, misas-
sembled regions, uneven sequence depths, varied alignment rates, and 
mapping failures and errors1,8. Additionally, the 25.92-Mb sheep Y chro-
mosome (chr) was recently updated in ARS-UI_Ramb_v3.0 (Ramb_v3.0)9; 
its divergence and structure are yet to be confirmed because of abun-
dant repeats10,11. However, a complete gap-free telomere-to-telomere 
(T2T) ovine genome has not been available until now.
In this study, we report the de novo T2T gap-free genome assembly 
for a ram (HU3095) of Hu sheep (T2T-sheep1.0), a well-known, highly 
prolific breed native to China. Additionally, we assembled the complete 
Y chromosome (T2T-sheep1.0-chrY) and the haplotype genome assem-
blies, paternal T2T-sheep1.0 (T2T-sheep1.0P) and maternal T2T-sheep1.0 
(T2T-sheep1.0M), at the T2T level. T2T-sheep1.0 was subsequently

219
Article
https://doi.org/10.1038/s41588-024-02037-6
T2T-sheep1.0 corrected many structural errors in Ramb_v3.0, for 
example, the INV195 inversion detected on chr09 (Fig. 1d and Extended 
Data Fig. 4). The alignment of the raw PacBio long reads generated from 
the Rambouillet and HU3095 sheep further revealed that the region 
covering INV195 was assembled correctly in T2T-sheep1.0, whereas an 
inversion error occurred in Ramb_v3.0, rather than a breed difference 
(Supplementary Information). The improvement in genome quality 
is also reflected in the average genome-wide quality value of 51.53 in 
T2T-sheep1.0, compared with 44.77 in Ramb_v3.0, excluding chrY (that 
is, Ramb_v2.0; GenBank accession GCA_016772045.1). Minimum unique 
k-mers (MUKs) are defined as the shortest sequence in a window occur-
ring only once in T2T-sheep1.0. Compared with Ramb_v3.0, T2T-sheep1.0 
presented an overall increase in the number of MUK sequences (Fig. 1e 
and Extended Data Fig. 5); centromeric and repetitive regions, for 
example, segmental duplications, presented longer MUKs than did 
the other chromosomal regions (Fig. 1a).
Genome annotation
We identified repetitive sequences in 47.67% (1,360.45 Mb) of 
T2T-sheep1.0 and 44.10% repetitive sequences (1,164.82 Mb) in Ramb_
v3.0 (Table 1). Among the repetitive sequences, most were transpos-
able elements (TEs), with a total size of 1,200.48 Mb, accounting for 
42.07% of T2T-sheep1.0. Long interspersed nuclear elements (LINEs) 
were the most abundant, spanning 780.1 Mb and covering 27.34% of 
T2T-sheep1.0. We assembled satellites with a total length of 162.14 Mb, 
covering 5.68% of T2T-sheep1.0. However, only 4.67 Mb of satellites 
(0.18% of the genome) were found in Ramb_v3.0, which was congruent 
with the high content of satellites in the PURs. The number of repeats 
in the PURs was much greater than that in the other chromosomal 
regions. Among PURs, 286,173 satellite sites (157.10 Mb) and 10,214 
segmental duplications (50.76 Mb) accounted for 96.89% of the satel-
lites and 19.65% of the segmental duplications in T2T-sheep1.0 (Sup-
plementary Table 6).
In total, 21,517 protein-coding genes were identified in 
T2T-sheep1.0, of which 754 were newly anchored genes located in 
the PURs (Table 1 and Supplementary Fig. 6). We annotated 99% of 
the protein-coding genes against public databases. We compared all 
the sequences between T2T-sheep1.0 and Ramb_v3.0, including all 
chromosomes and unassembled contigs, and identified 208.85 Mb 
of newly assembled regions and 712 newly assembled genes (NAGs) 
in T2T-sheep1.0 that were not included in Ramb_v3.0 (Supplementary 
Fig. 6). We identified 147 genes in the centromeric regions of 25 chro-
mosomes in T2T-sheep1.0; RNA sequencing (RNA-seq) analysis revealed 
low expression levels of these genes (Supplementary Tables 7 and 8).
For the 18,084 orthogroups, we observed obvious expansion of 
gene families with increased copy numbers in T2T-sheep1.0 compared 
with those in the sheep Ramb_v3.0, argali CAU_O.ammon polii_1.0 (Ovis 
ammon polii) and goat ARS1 (Capra hircus) (Supplementary Table 9). 
Furthermore, gene family expansion was strongly associated with the 
enrichment of segmental duplications (Supplementary Fig. 7). We 
identified 258.32 Mb and 66.62 Mb of nonredundant segmental dupli-
cated sequences in T2T-sheep1.0 and Ramb_v3.0, respectively (Sup-
plementary Information). A total of 45.56% of the interchromosomal 
segmental duplications were scattered across different chromosomes, 
whereas 54.44% of the intrachromosomal segmental duplications were 
identified in T2T-sheep1.0.
Centromeric regions and their repeat contents
Eleven acrocentric chromosomes shared similar centromeric satel-
lite sequences; an assembly graph revealed the tangles among them 
(Fig. 2a). After gap filling, centromeric regions were resolved with the 
support of sufficient read coverage, including tangled regions (Fig. 1a 
and Supplementary Fig. 1). As histone H3 binds to centromeres on 
nucleosomes, we determined the centromeric regions using chromatin 
immunoprecipitation followed by sequencing (ChIP–seq) (Fig. 2b). 
used as a reference to investigate genomic components, particularly 
centromere and telomere structures, previously unresolved regions 
(PURs), new genes, structural variants (SVs) and single-nucleotide 
polymorphisms (SNPs). Furthermore, application of T2T-sheep1.0 to 
the analysis of wild and domestic sheep populations worldwide has 
advantages over previous assemblies with regard to variant calling, 
population analyses and identification of variants related to domes-
tication and selection for wool fineness.
Results
T2T gap-free genome assembly
In total, 543.2 Gb of ultralong Oxford Nanopore Technology 
(ONT) reads (189.0×) and 149.0 Gb of Pacific Biosciences (PacBio) 
HiFi reads (52.0×) were obtained to assemble T2T-sheep1.0 (Sup-
plementary Table 1, Extended Data Fig. 1 and Supplementary 
Methods). The initial assembly consisted of 246 contigs whose 
N50 reached 96.54 Mb (Supplementary Table 2). Furthermore, 
1135.86 Gb of optical genome mapping (OGM) data and 357.22 Gb 
of high-throughput chromatin capture (Hi-C) data (Supplementary 
Table 1) were used to scaffold the contigs and anchor them onto 
27 pseudomolecules, that is, 26 autosomes and chrX. Alignments 
of OGM data and reads of ONT, HiFi and Hi-C indicated that all 139 
gaps enriched mostly in the centromeric regions were filled by 
ultralong ONT reads (Fig. 1a, Supplementary Table 3, Extended 
Data Fig. 2 and Supplementary Fig. 1).
ChrY was assembled independently based on paternal-specific 
ONT and HiFi reads. All 56 telomeric regions for the 28 chromosomes 
ranged from 1.20 to 25.32 kb (Fig. 1a and Supplementary Fig. 2). Finally, 
the complete genome assembly of T2T-sheep1.0 was constructed 
with a size of 2.85 Gb (Table 1), including 26 autosomes, chrX and 
chrY. Additionally, a trio-based assembly was performed to obtain 
haplotype-resolved autosomes for T2T-sheep1.0P and T2T-sheep1.0M. 
Because of the lack of binned long reads, four and ten gaps in the cen-
tromeric or pericentromeric regions of two (chr25 and chr26) and six 
(chr10, chr13, chr17, chr23, chr25 and chr26) chromosomes were left for 
T2T-sheep1.0P and T2T-sheep1.0M, respectively. Finally, the complete 
chrX and chrY in T2T-sheep1.0 were included in T2T-sheep1.0M and 
T2T-sheep1.0P, respectively (Table 1).
After polishing, the consensus base quality values across 
T2T-sheep1.0, T2T-sheep1.0P and T2T-sheep1.0M were 51.53, 55.41 and 
55.23, respectively (>99.999% base accuracy) (Supplementary Table 4). 
We obtained 99.75%, 98.99% and 99.97% mapping rates for short, ONT 
and HiFi reads, respectively, against T2T-sheep1.0. The even coverage 
distribution of ONT and PacBio HiFi reads suggested three reliable and 
continuous assemblies (Fig. 1a, Extended Data Fig. 3 and Supplemen-
tary Fig. 1). We calculated the switching errors and obtained estimates 
of 0.3781‰, 0.0895‰ and 0.1199‰ for T2T-sheep1.0, T2T-sheep1.0P and 
T2T-sheep1.0M, respectively. Heterozygous regions, such as 9,982,198 
single-nucleotide variants, 1,248,272 small insertions and deletions 
(<50 bp) and 20,155 SVs (≥50 bp) were detected between T2T-sheep1.0P 
and T2T-sheep1.0M (Extended Data Fig. 3).
Good collinearity was detected between T2T-sheep1.0 and Ramb_
v3.0 (GenBank accession GCA_016772045.2). PURs unassembled (that 
is, gaps) or misassembled on the chromosomes of Ramb_v3.0 span 
nearly 220.05 Mb on all 28 chromosomes of T2T-sheep1.0 (Fig. 1a, 
Supplementary Figs. 1 and 3, and Supplementary Information). The 
PURs included centromeric satellites (CenSats) (60.72%), segmental 
duplications (12.95%), overlapping CenSat and segmental duplications 
(10.06%), and other repeats (8.15%) (Fig. 1b). Some genes (for example, 
gene 1808, that is, HNRNPK) lost in Ramb_v3.0 were annotated in the 
gap-filled regions of T2T-sheep1.0 (Fig. 1c and Supplementary Fig. 4). 
Compared with 26 other ovine genome assemblies, T2T-sheep1.0 exhib-
ited increased genome length, Benchmarking Universal Single-Copy 
Orthologs (BUSCO) and centromeric satellite content (Supplementary 
Fig. 5, Supplementary Table 5 and Supplementary Information).

the successful alignment of two known centromere-specific satellite 
DNA sequences (GenBank accessions KM272303.1 and U24091). The

Fig. 1 | Genomic comparisons of the Ramb_v3.0 and T2T-sheep1.0 sheep 
assemblies. a, Genomic features annotated on chr01 for Ramb_v3.0 and T2T-
sheep1.0. The coverages of ultralong ONT (cov. ONT) and PacBio HiFi (cov. 
PacBio) long reads are shown in 200-kb windows. Gene, SD and TE density values 
were calculated in 10-kb windows. MUK indicates the minimum unique k-mer 
length in 100-kb windows. Error k-mer indicates 21-mer errors caused by the 
worse assembly. PUR indicates a PUR in T2T-sheep1.0 compared to Ramb_v3.0. 
The gray blocks between the two horizontal gray bars of T2T-sheep1.0 and 
Ramb_v3.0 indicate collinearity; one inversion and two duplications between 
the two assemblies are plotted in orange and blue, respectively. Centromeres 
are highlighted in dark blue, telomeres are marked with black triangles on the 
T2T-sheep1.0 gray bar, and gaps are shown in green on the Ramb_v3.0 gray bar. 
b, Contents of several sequence types in the PURs of T2T-sheep1.0 compared to 
Ramb_v3.0. CenSat indicates satellites in centromeric regions identified using 
RepeatMasker. RepMask indicates other repeats identified by RepeatMasker. 
c, One gap containing a gene (gene 1808, that is, HNRNPK) with transcriptional 
expression on chr01 of Ramb_v3.0 was filled in T2T-sheep1.0. Genes colored in 
yellow show transcriptional expression according to RNA-seq in 10-kb windows in 
tissues from the longissimus dorsi, cerebrum and hypothalamus. The coverage of 
ONT and PacBio HiFi reads confirmed the reliability of gap filling. d, An inversion 
error (INV195), highlighted in orange, was found on chr09 in Ramb_v3.0 and 
corrected in T2T-sheep1.0. The genes in the region were expressed in tissues from 
the hypothalamus, ileum and cerebrum. Two peaks of error k-mers (k = 21) were 
found in Ramb_v3.0 and correspond to the two junction sites of this false-positive 
inversion, which could not be covered by PacBio reads from Rambouillet sheep 
(Sequence Read Archive accessions SRX3445660–SRX3445663) assembled 
previously for Ramb_v3.0. e, Genome-wide MUK lengths in a comparison of T2T-
sheep1.0 and Ramb_v3.0.

Nature Genetics | Volume 57 | January 2025 | 218–230
221
Article
https://doi.org/10.1038/s41588-024-02037-6
hypermethylated regions covered the entire centromeric region; 
hypomethylation displayed a centromeric dip region correspond-
ing to SatII (one type of centromeric satellite) on chr02 (Fig. 2b). Cen-
tromeric dip regions, which have also been reported in humans12,14 
and medaka15, could be associated with interactions with centromere 
protein A (CENP-A) for the binding of functional kinetochores and 
served as distinct markers for euchromatin16–18. Heatmaps of pairwise 
sequence identity confirmed centromeric locations with continuous 
blocks (Fig. 2b); the sequence complexity signals or entropies exhib-
ited different distributions in centromeric regions from those in other 
regions (Fig. 2b, Extended Data Fig. 6 and Supplementary Fig. 8). The 
lines of evidence from enriched signals of ChIP–seq, hypermethylation 
and satellite DNA alignment confirmed that the centromeric regions 
had lengths varying from 0.36 Mb to 22.63 Mb (Fig. 2c and Extended 
Data Fig. 6).
Centromeric satellite DNA that consisted of higher-order repeats 
(HORs) was classified into three categories: SatI, SatII and SatIII (Sup-
plementary Methods and Fig. 2c). We confirmed the similarity of SatI 
(816 bp) to the NCBI satellite sequence KM272303.1 (ref. 19) and deter-
mined the size of SatII to be 702 bp instead of approximately 400 bp 
of the NCBI satellite sequence U24091 (ref. 20). We revealed the third 
satellite unit, SatIII (22 bp), and validated it using fluorescence in situ 
hybridization (FISH) assays (Fig. 2d). Additionally, we detected the 
insertion of other repeats, such as LINEs and short interspersed ele-
ments (SINEs), into centromeric regions (Fig. 2b). In contrast to genes 
within centromeric regions, genes in pericentromeric regions were 
highly expressed according to the RNA-seq data (Fig. 2b).
Evolution of centromeric satellites and chromosomal fusion
Sheep have experienced evolutionary events involving the fusion of 
two acrocentric chromosomes into one metacentric chromosome, 
which occurred on chr01, chr02 and chr03 (ref. 20). The collinearity 
among the chromosomes of sheep, argali4 and goat21 revealed 2:1 fusion 
relationships (for example, CHI1 + CHI3 in goat versus chr01 in sheep; 
CHI2 + CHI8 in goat versus chr02 in sheep; and CHI5 + CHI11 in goat 
versus chr03 in sheep) (Fig. 2e). In primates, centromeres evolved via 
the amplification of satellite sequences in their inner cores, forming 
layers of varying ages22. Like the presentation of centromeric regions in 
humans12, we also observed the evolutionary layers of centromeric sat-
ellites based on sequence identity and similarity heatmaps (Extended 
Data Fig. 6). The four evolutionary layers of centromeric satellites on 
chr02, including layers 1 and 2 of SatI and layers 3 and 4 of SatII (Fig. 2b) 
based on identity values of 80–100%, suggested multiple amplifica-
tion events of SatI and SatII. We did not detect telomeric sequences 
in the centromeric regions of these three metacentric chromosomes 
with fusion events, which is different from previous observations in 
muntjac deer23.
Multiple sequence alignment of SatI revealed 599 variant sites 
in the 16 Bovidae species and 86 variants between T2T-sheep1.0 
and T2T-goat1.0 (ref. 21) (goat T2T genome, GenBank accession 
GCA_040806595.1; Supplementary Table 10 and Supplementary 
Fig. 9a). A phylogenetic tree based on SatI consensus sequences 
revealed a split of Caprinae and Hippotraginae species in Bovidae 
(Fig. 2f), suggesting the conservation of SatI. The sequences of SatII 
harbored 130 variant sites between sheep and goat (Supplementary

Fig. 2 | Assembly of centromeric regions and identification of centromeric 
repeat units. a, An assembly graph tangle among the centromeres of 11 
acrocentric chromosomes. Centromeric regions are highlighted in yellow.  
b, Genomic features of the centromeric region on chr02. ChIP–seq for the 
histone H3 variant CENP-A (phospho-CENP-A (Ser7) antibody), methylation 
based on HiFi reads and satellite enrichment were used to identify centromeric 
regions. The three types of repeats (LINEs, SINEs and satellites) are shown in the 
‘repeats’ bar; two satellite units (SatI and SatII) occupy the centromeric region in 
the ‘satellites’ bar. Bottom, the sequence identity heatmap with a color scale (left 
bottom corner) is shown across the centromere in nonoverlapping 5-kb windows; 
four evolutionary layers corresponding to two layers (1 and 2) of SatI and two 
layers (3 and 4) of SatII in the centromeric region are marked from 1 to 4. c, Top, 
chromosome CRUs. Bottom, lengths of chromosomes. d, FISH images for the 
probes of SatIII and CenY. Two SatIII variants were identified in the T2T genome 
assemblies of both sheep (SatIII-20GG and SatIII-20CC) and goat (SatIII-V2A 
and SatIII-V2C in T2T-goat1.0); their sequences are aligned below. The probes of 
SatIII-20GG (red) and SatIII-20CC (green) were also used for FISH imaging (right), 
while the probes of CenY (red) and SatIII (combining SatIII-20GG and SatIII-20CC, 
green) were hybridized onto the sheep chromosomes in FISH (left). The chrY 
and CenY probes (red) in the middle of the left plot have been enlarged in a white 
line box at the top right corner. The experiments were repeated independently 
at least three times with similar results. e, Collinearity of three metacentric 
chromosomes (chr1, chr2 and chr3) among T2T-sheep1.0, T2T-goat1.0 and 
argali (Ovis ammon polii). SatI, SatII and SatIII are colored for their presence or 
absence in the centromeres. f, Phylogenetic tree of 15 species based on their SatI 
sequences (Supplementary Table 10).

Fig. 3 | Assembly of chrX and chrY. a, Collinearity of chrY between T2T-sheep1.0 
and Ramb_v3.0, that is, T2T-sheep1.0-chrY versus Ramb_v3.0-chrY. Top right, the 
colored sequence identity scale. b, Genomic features of T2T-sheep1.0-chrY. From 
top to bottom, chrY consists of the X homologous region (PAR) and the male-
specific region (MSY), with the CenY (blue) and Z zone (red) labeled; densities 
of TEs, LINEs, SINEs, LTRs and satellites, with scales at the bottom left corner; 
methylated cytosines in 10-kb windows based on HiFi (red) and ONT (light red) 
data; coverage of PacBio HiFi reads (cov. PacBio); segmental duplication (SD) 
density in 10-kb windows; gene distribution; three multicopy genes, that is, HSFY, 
TSPY and ZFY; pseudogenes, with the ones in the Z zone highlighted in red; gene 
expression from RNA-seq of testis and blood tissues; coverage of ONT reads (cov. 
ONT); sequence identity across the whole chrY, showing the high-identity signals 
of CenY (magnified bottom left) and the Z zone. c, Genomic features annotated 
on chrX and regions homologous to chrY. Homologous genes between chrX 
and chrY are connected by light blue lines. The color keys are the same as those 
in Fig. 1a. d, Two inversion errors (INV405 and INV406) on chrX of Ramb_v3.0, 
which were corrected in T2T-sheep1.0. The gray blocks between the horizontal 
bars of T2T-sheep1.0 and Ramb_v3.0 represent collinear regions. The orange lines 
indicate inversions on chrX between Ramb_v3.0 and T2T-sheep1.0. There was no 
coverage of Rambouillet PacBio reads downloaded from the NCBI (BioSample 
ID: SAMN17575729) at the two junction sites of each inversion in Ramb_v3.0, in 
contrast to the accurate assembly and uniform coverage of HiFi reads from Hu 
sheep in T2T-sheep1.0.

Fig. 9b), while two SatIII variants (SatIII-20GG and SatIII-20CC for 
T2T-sheep1.0, and SatIII-V2A and SatIII-V2C for T2T-goat1.0) were found 
for both species (Fig. 2d). The FISH results revealed that the SatIII vari-
ants (SatIII-20GG and SatIII-20CC) both presented intensified signals 
at the chromosomal ends (Fig. 2d).
Structure of chrY
Because of the homology of chrX and chrY10, that is, the pseudoau-
tosomal regions (PARs), chrY assembly is challenging (Supplemen-
tary Fig. 10a). Recently, gap-free T2T Y chromosomes were released 
in humans24, six apes25 and sheep9 (chrY in Ramb_v3.0, that is, Ramb_
v3.0-chrY). In this study, T2T-sheep1.0-chrY had a length of 26.59 Mb, 
which is 0.67 Mb and 15.97 Mb longer than the Y chromosomes of 
Ramb_v3.0 and the earlier Hu sheep genome ASM1117029v1 (ref. 10) 
(GenBank accession GCA_011170295.1), respectively. T2T-sheep1.0-chrY 
showed good collinearity in the approximately 17-Mb regions compared 
with Ramb_v3.0-chrY, except for a 1.18-Mb inversion in the middle and 
small inversions in the approximately 9-Mb region (that is, the Z zone) 
at the end of T2T-sheep1.0-chrY distal to the PAR (Fig. 3a).
Centromere-specific satellites (SatI, SatII and SatIII) were not 
observed, but another repeat unit CenY (2,516 bp) spanned a total 
of 180.12 kb uniquely on T2T-sheep1.0-chrY. FISH probes for CenY 
showed unique binding to chrY (Fig. 2d). This observation is con-
gruent with the lack of hybridization of the SatI and SatII probes to 
chrY in FISH assays of sheep20,26. As a potential centromeric repeat 
unit (CRU) on chrY, CenY was supported by hypermethylation and 
high sequence identity (Fig. 3b). A repeat sequence (1,473 bp) similar 
to CenY was detected on goat chrY21; CenY was also found in other 
Bovidae species, such as Bos taurus (GenBank accessions CP128563.1 
and LR962769.1). LINEs, SINEs and long terminal repeats (LTRs) were 
detected on T2T-sheep1.0-chrY, and accounted for 39.72, 6.20 and 
7.24%, respectively.
A total of 133 protein-coding genes and 59 pseudogenes were 
annotated on T2T-sheep1.0-chrY; these were supported by RNA-seq

of mapped reads and the error rate for a ratio between mismatches and bases
