passing QC in two or more populations (Fig. 3a and Supplementary 
Figs. 27–29). Overall, we pruned 16,528 ancestry–trait pairs with 
available GWAS to 1,091 ancestry–trait pairs that passed all filters 
spanning 452 traits (Fig. 3a). Of the ancestry–trait GWAS pairs that 
passed, most were shared between the two largest ancestry groups 
(EUR and CSA), with 147 phenotypes found in three or more genetic 
ancestry groups (Supplementary Fig. 29). As many phenotypes in 
the UKB are correlated with each other, we pruned to a maximal set 
of independent phenotypes with pairwise r2 < 0.1, resulting in a set of 
151 phenotypes (Supplementary Fig. 30), and computed polygenicity 
estimates for all high-quality traits (Supplementary Fig. 31). We note 
that these genome-wide summaries aid in prioritizing phenotypes 
with broad heritable components, although the failure of a pheno-
type in this framework does not necessarily preclude true individual 
SNP-level associations.
Among high-quality GWAS results that passed our broad-scale QC 
of GWAS results, we identified consistency in the relative magnitude 
of heritability estimates among populations. For example, 113 inde-
pendent phenotypes passed QC in both EUR and CSA genetic ancestry 
groups, and the heritability estimates for these traits had a significant 
positive correlation (York regression slope = 0.49, P = 5 × 10−12; Fig. 3b 
and Extended Data Fig. 2c); the heritability estimates in this set were 
systematically higher in the CSA, probably reflecting a combination of 
winner’s curse from selection of phenotypes with significant estimates 
in CSA and methodological differences between S-LDSC and RHE-mc 
estimates, including how they handle effects of any residual popula-
tion stratification. Biomarkers and continuous phenotypes tended to 
have the highest heritability estimates (EUR average h2 = 0.19 and 0.16), 
compared to lower estimates for disease and prescription phenotypes 
(EUR average h2 = 0.021 and 0.016; Extended Data Fig. 3b).
Genomic discoveries powered by Pan-UKB analysis
We next investigated the extent to which broader inclusion of ances-
trally diverse participants identifies specific biological signals through 
meta-analysis, compared to EUR-only analysis. In particular, for 452 
high-quality phenotypes, we compared P values for the multiancestry 
meta-analysis to those from the EUR GWAS, which were overall highly 
correlated (Fig. 4a and Supplementary Fig. 32; r2 = 0.999; P < 10−100).

We identified 237,360 significant (P < 5 × 10−8), LD-independent asso-
ciations in the largest meta-analysis available across 431 phenotypes, 
with LD defined as above (Supplementary Fig. 20). Of these, 14,676 
(6.2%) were not significant in EUR, representing new biology discovered 
solely by analyzing already available data from diverse populations.
To compare to previous trait-specific and systematic analyses in 
the UKB40,44 and other datasets, we created a framework to quantify 
associations added by our analysis compared to existing associations in 
Open Targets Genetics48. Two challenges with phenome-wide compari-
sons is the pervasiveness of pleiotropy coupled with mapping pheno-
types across GWASs, which may be coded slightly differently and have 
subtle differences in phenotype definitions49. Through semi-manual 
curation, we mapped 3,047 (42%) of our traits to terms in the experi-
mental factor ontology (EFO), of which 2,566 matched a study in the 
GWAS catalog; our ability to cross-reference phenotypes varied by trait 
type (Supplementary Table 10). Furthermore, as LD-independent asso-
ciations are challenging (for example, even r2 < 0.1 can result in spurious 
‘independent’ signals when in LD with extremely significant signals with 
χ2 > 300), we computed distance-based independent associations to 
assess novelty conservatively, and compared to known associations 
accordingly. Specifically, we defined a variant as new only if no known 
associations for the same EFO broad category are present within 1 Mb. 
We identified 71,372 regionally independent associations that mapped 
to an EFO category, of which 68,260 (96%) were reported previously for 
the same EFO category. A total of 3,112 (4%) were not identified previ-
ously (Fig. 4b), with this rate varying by EFO category (Extended Data 
Fig. 4). The X chromosome contributes an outsized proportion of this 
novelty, with 573 of 2,448 (23%) associations not reported previously 
for the same EFO category, probably due to exclusion of the X chromo-
some in many previous GWASs50.
Newly significant associations arise due to a combination of fac-
tors: bolstered associations that crossed the genome-wide significance 
threshold with mixed models from previously subthreshold associa-
tions in standard regression, increased sample size of EUR participants 
and inclusion of participants with non-EUR ancestries that either added 
support or contributed outsized significance due to ancestry-enriched 
variation. Of the 3,112 new meta-analysis associations, 3,092 show sug-
gestive signals (for example, 5 × 10−8 < P < 10−6) in EUR-only analyses

where the multiancestry meta-analysis results in genome-wide sig-
nificance (P < 5 × 10−8). Allele frequencies were enriched by at least 
tenfold in at least one genetic ancestry group over EUR in at least one 
genetic ancestry group for 260 associations (247 both suggestive in 
EUR and enriched outside EUR; Fig. 4b). For instance, we find a sig-
nificant association between triglycerides and CAMK2D (meta-analysis 
P = 1.5 × 10−8; EUR P = 0.0017; Fig. 4c) at a variant (rs193059864) that 
is 114-fold enriched in AFR (AFR frequency 1.6%, EUR frequency 
1.4 × 10−4). Another variant in this gene has recently been implicated 
in heart failure51; however, rs193059864 is in low LD with the variant 
identified in this study (rs17620390; r2 = 0.001), indicating that these 
probably represent independent associations.
We investigated broad patterns of gene function closest to 
each association to assess biological relevance. We find that 66% 
(124 of 187) of haploinsufficient genes are near a new significant asso-
ciation (Extended Data Fig. 5a and Supplementary Fig. 33), compared 
to 34% of all genes (5,969 of 17,428). Associations near haploinsuf-
ficient genes often correspond to broadly similar phenotype catego-
ries as the Online Mendelian Inheritance in Man annotation of the 
gene. For instance, we find associations between SNP rs77767351 (near 
PITX2) and several keratometry measurements, including 3-mm weak 
meridian-right (meta-analysis P = 1.2 × 10−10; Fig. 4d). Previous studies 
have identified a crucial role for PITX2 in embryonic development and 
tissue formation52 and implicated mutations in this gene with rare 
Mendelian eye-related diseases, such as Axenfeld–Rieger syndrome53,54. 
To our knowledge, this SNP has not been associated significantly in 
any GWAS and indicates a potential allelic series: the intermediate eye 
phenotype associated with the common variant can provide context 
for the molecular mechanisms underlying the dominant condition 
caused by high-impact variants in PITX2.
Inclusion of several genetic ancestries improves GWAS
To further explore potential reasons for associations significant only 
in the meta-analysis, we compared effect sizes to allele frequencies by 
ancestry. For quantitative traits where effect sizes are directly com-
parable, we broadly observed the characteristic inverse relationship 
between minor allele frequency (MAF) and effect size, in which rarer 
variants tend to have larger effect sizes than common variants55,56 
(Fig. 5a). We identified genome-wide significant associations through 
meta-analysis and not in the EUR GWAS in some cases due to higher 
ancestry-specific MAFs, which rendered some variants with larger effect 
sizes accessible to discovery despite the smaller sample size (Fig. 5b), 
although winner’s curse may inflate some effect size estimates57.
We thus investigated the most extreme differences—specifically, 
associations significant in the meta-analysis, but not in the EUR GWAS 
(that is, those in the upper left quadrant of Fig. 4a). We find that these 
associations were more likely to be found at high-quality variants 
(Supplementary Note) and were sixfold enriched for variants more 
common in AFR (fourfold in any non-EUR ancestry; Extended Data 
Fig. 6), indicating that these associations are probably enriched for 
universally tagging or putatively causal variants in several popula-
tions. The most extreme differences in P values stemmed from extreme 
frequency differences, such as between variants in/near G6PD and a 
number of traits (Fig. 6), where a higher frequency in AFR increased 
power for association. However, perhaps paradoxically, variants with 
P values more significant in EUR-only GWAS compared to meta-analysis 
were also enriched 2.4-fold for those with higher AFR frequencies 
(Extended Data Fig. 6). These variants are most likely not causal, but 
tagging variants with differential LD patterns across ancestries leading 
to apparent heterogeneity in meta-analysis. For instance, a variant in 
LD with a causal variant in EUR but only partial LD in AFR with increased 
frequency will be identified as heterogeneous with little or no effect in 
AFR. Indeed, heterogeneity (Cochran’s Q P < 0.01) was enriched 2.5-fold 
for variants with reduced significance in the meta-analysis compared to 
the EUR GWAS alone (Extended Data Fig. 6 and Supplementary Fig. 32).
Ancestry-enriched variation refines multitrait associations
Among the most extreme differences we identified between the 
meta-analysis and EUR-only results was a missense variant (rs1050828) 
in G6PD, which had significant associations with five phenotypes in 
the AFR group (allele frequency, 16%; Fig. 6a) but not in EUR (allele 
frequency, 1.5 × 10−4), including glycated hemoglobin (HbA1c; Fig. 6b), 
RET (high light scatter reticulocyte count), red blood cell (erythrocyte) 
count (RBC), red blood cell distribution width (RDW) and mean sphered 
cell volume (MSCV) (Fig. 6c, Supplementary Fig. 34 and Supplementary 
Table 11). Our results replicate previous associations between variants 
in/near G6PD and HbA1c58, and further validate the pleiotropic effect of 
this missense variant. We performed ancestry-specific fine-mapping 
of these signals (Extended Data Fig. 7a), which confirm the likely func-
tional nature of this variant; however, fine-mapping of meta-analyzed 
summary statistics presented a challenge (Extended Data Fig. 7b). 
Specifically, the high frequency of rs1050828 and rs201543735 in AFR 
(MAF = 0.173 and MAF = 0.176) versus low frequency in the meta-analysis 
(MAF < 0.01) contribute to completely opposite fine-mapping conclu-
sions for HbA1c: PIP = 1 and 0.944 in AFR versus PIP = 0 for both SNPs 
in the meta-analysis. In contrast, all six of the SNPs with PIP = 1 in the 
meta-analysis have PIP ≅ 0 in AFR despite an allele frequency close to 
0 in the meta-analysis but common (MAF > 0.05) in AFR. Consistent 
with conclusions from previous studies59, these findings highlight that 
statistical fine-mapping in meta-analysis can result in widely varying 
interpretations due to technical challenges as well as imbalance in 
sample size, ancestry and allele frequencies.
To further explore the influence of ancestral haplotypes on trait 
associations, we performed a pilot study running Tractor60 for a subset 
of traits on a set of participants similar to those in the AFR SAIGE tests. 
We find further accurate group-level effect size estimation compared 
to SAIGE (Extended Data Fig. 8, Supplementary Data 7 and Supplemen-
tary Note), highlighting the utility of further refining ancestry to more 
robustly evaluate genetic associations with traits.
Discussion
We present a multiancestry genetic study and resource that extends 
previous analyses focused only on EUR participants to a wider set of

ancestrally diverse UKB participants. Here we build a new framework, 
including best practices for multiphenotype multiancestry analysis, 
that we recommend be adopted by groups performing similar analyses 
for other biobanks. Our QC framework provides a blueprint to identify 
phenotypes with robust GWAS, particularly when sample sizes are 
imbalanced and/or with stratification. Our results show that diverse 
analysis is critical for maximizing biological discovery even with imbal-
anced sample sizes, and that full data release is critical to broad com-
parisons across datasets. We provide summary statistics for 16,528 
GWASs, as well as LD scores/matrices, and sample metadata returned 
to the UKB to reduce the barrier for future multiancestry analyses of 
these existing and new phenotypes. Indeed, this work contributed 
continuously updated summary statistics to the COVID-19 Host Genet-
ics Initiative43, as well as broader efforts such as the Global Biobank 
Meta-analysis Initiative61.
In this work, we highlight challenges in performing multiphe-
notype analyses of thousands of phenotypes, particularly around 
quantifying novelty and fine-mapping in the presence of imbalanced 
sample sizes. We identify 3,112 genome-wide significant associations 
not found in Open Targets Genetics48. These discoveries arise from 
ancestry-enriched variants (for example, CAMK2D rs193059864 with 
frequency enriched in AFR) as well as previously subsignificant sig-
nals (5 × 10−8 < P < 10−6; for example, rs77767351 in PITX2), which are 
associated significantly here due to a combination of larger sample 
sizes in the European ancestry group, broader ancestry inclusion, and 
use of advanced mixed models that increase statistical power. Some 
associations demonstrate allelic series, in which variants in the same 
gene have different levels of effects on related phenotypes: common 
variants in PITX2 are associated with variation in traits (keratometry 
traits), whereas rare pLoF variants in ClinVar are related to severe

Nature Genetics | Volume 57 | October 2025 | 2408–2417
2415
Article
https://doi.org/10.1038/s41588-025-02335-7
diseases (for example, Axenfeld–Rieger syndrome). Another challenge 
when evaluating novelty across ancestries with imbalanced sample 
sizes is defining loci, particularly given differences in LD. Although we 
performed LD clumping, well-powered GWAS with highly significant 
loci often report a questionable number of secondary ‘independent’ 
associations within a region. Alternative approaches for identifying 
truly independent loci—particularly in cases with large effects and 
long-range LD—include conditional analyses or a dynamic locus defi-
nition based on LD and primary signal strength (for example, pruning 
variants with r2 > 10/χ2 to the primary association62).
Furthermore, we highlight a set of pleiotropic associations for a 
common missense variant in G6PD in the AFR genetic ancestry group 
that is rare and thus inaccessible in other genetic ancestry groups. 
Whereas some of these phenotypes were filtered out by our QC pipe-
line in AFR, we note that these broad filters do not preclude individual 
true associations. Fine-mapping of this locus in AFR identifies two 
plausible variants in credible sets, including rs1050828, which has been 
validated clinically to reduce HbA1c levels and increase diabetes com-
plications, such as diabetic retinopathy63. In contrast, fine-mapping 
across a meta-analyzed cohort leads to instability, particularly in this 
case where a smaller ancestry group contributes an outsized fraction of 
the variance explained (that is, 2pq × β2, where p is the allele frequency 
and q is 1 − p). These results demonstrate a clear need for cohorts with 
more balanced sample sizes across ancestry groups, such as the All of 
Us Research Program cohort64. Diverse cohorts will necessitate careful 
GWAS methodology, as statistical assumptions of homogeneity are 
often violated in recently admixed genomes, which may be addressed 
by scaling methods that consider recent admixture60. Furthermore, 
diverse cohorts provide new opportunities to understand hetero-
geneity in effects and to characterize the influence of variable allele 
frequency, LD patterns and environmental factors. Despite the chal-
lenges in analyzing diverse GWAS data, it is scientifically imperative 
to accurately identify new associations, resolve causal variants and 
increase accuracy in polygenic score analysis for all.
In our study, we assigned individual participants into distinct 
ancestry groups. This is a pragmatic approach for statistical analysis 
that reflects legitimate tradeoffs between controlling type 1 error due 
to population stratification using meta-analysis versus enhancing 
statistical power in mega-analyses, which we evaluated empirically. 
The ideal strategy has not been adjudicated fully in the field, as it is 
rare that both approaches are equally feasible. Previous studies have 
shown that results from both approaches are highly comparable65,66. 
We find outsized benefits to meta-analysis in controlling type 1 error, 
which is especially important when analyzing thousands of traits 
with widely varying degrees of stratification, and an added benefit 
is providing ancestry-specific effect size estimates from GWAS that 
enables more straightforward downstream comparative analyses of 
genetic effects with datasets from different ancestries. However, this 
approach simplifies the complex and continuous concept of ancestry 
into discrete categories and thus risks reifying incorrect assumptions 
about biological essentialism to racial and ethnic groupings (see Sup-
plementary Note FAQ). Readers should be cautious not to infer that 
these ancestry groups reflect rigid or discrete biological entities; the 
boundaries between assigned population identifiers are arbitrary and 
do not necessarily align with biological realities, but rather should 
be viewed simply as a methodological convenience. In the future, 
methods that use continuous measures of genetic diversity without 
increasing false positives may provide additional insights and avoid 
oversimplification.
Moreover, the biology of causal variants is shared mostly across 
populations10. In GWAS, differences in MAF and LD due to distinct 
demographic histories across populations drives differences in statis-
tical power, given that most top loci are tagging variants rather than 
causal. Our detection of GWAS loci unique to particular populations 
are thus most likely due to improved statistical power for detection in 
that population, rather than a true causal variant with distinct effects 
across ancestry backgrounds. GWAS associations identified in only 
one ancestry group should thus not be taken as evidence of biological 
differences between groups. By including several ancestral popula-
tions in our analyses, we empower identification of loci meaningful 
to everyone’s health, regardless of ancestry.
Conducting systematic analyses across a massive range of traits 
raises sensitivities that users of this resource will attempt to make 
inappropriate comparisons across ancestry groups. Previous work 
has shown that comparing polygenic score distributions across ances-
tries is not scientifically meaningful, and we discourage use of this 
resource to make trait comparisons on the basis of race or ethnicity. 
We encourage consulting a resource of FAQs we developed as part of 
this project (https://pan.ukbb.broadinstitute.org/) when using this 
resource to make any population-based comparisons and evaluating 
risks versus benefits.

butions and competing interests; and statements of data and code avail-

Passenger mutations link cellular origin 
and transcriptional identity in human lung 
adenocarcinomas
 
Sukanya Panja1,2,8, Padmaja Mantri1,2,8, Kofi Ennu Johnson2,3,4,8, 
Juan Sebastian Andrade-Martinez 
  1,2,3,4,8, Soo-Ryum Yang5, Aditya Deshpande1,2, 
Huasong Tian1,2, Shaham Beg 
  4, Kentaro Ohara4, Alessandro Leal 
  1, 
Joel Rosiene 
  2,4, Marlon Stoeckius2, Peter Smibert 
  2, William D. Travis5, 
Juan Miguel Mosquera 
  4, Paz Polak6,7 & Marcin Imieliński 
  1,2 
DNA damage is preferentially repaired in expressed genes; thus, 
genome-wide correlations between somatic mutation patterns and normal 
cell transcription may reflect tumor cell origins. Accordingly, we found 
that aggregate lung adenocarcinoma (LUAD) and squamous cancer (LUSC) 
somatic mutation density associated most strongly with distal (alveolar) 
and proximal (basal) lung cell-type-specific gene expression, respectively, 
consistent with presumed LUAD and LUSC cell origins. Analyzing individual 
genomes, 21% of LUADs bore mutational footprints of proximal airway 
origins, with 38% classified as ambiguous. Distal origin LUADs, enriched 
for KRAS and STK11 drivers, occurred mainly in smokers; proximal origin 
LUADs, enriched for EGFR drivers, were more common in never-smokers. 
Ambiguous origin LUADs showed APOBEC signatures and SMARCA4 
alterations. TP53 mutant LUADs with non-distal cell origins preferentially 
exhibited non-distal transcriptional identity. Our study reveals a complex 
interplay between lineage and identity in LUAD evolution and offers a 
scalable strategy to infer tumor origins in human cancers.
The cancer cell-of-origin (COO), the normal cell type from which a 
cancer originates, is fundamental to our understanding of carcino-
genesis1. Working hypotheses of human cancer COOs are derived from 
the resemblance of malignant to benign cells, whether on the basis of 
morphology, protein expression, methylation or gene expression.
For example, non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) is subdivided 
into LUAD and LUSC based on the respective similarity of cancer cells 
to the glandular epithelium of distal airways (LUAD, alveolar type 2 
(AT2) cells) or stratified keratinized epithelium of proximal bronchi 
(LUSC, basal cells). Concordantly, transformation of AT2 and basal 
cells gives rise to LUAD and LUSC tumors, respectively, in genetically 
engineered mouse models (GEMMs)2,3.
Many cancers (including LUADs) show clinically significant lineage 
plasticity after treatment4,5. Pre-treatment LUADs are also histologically 
pleiotropic, with 80% showing two or more distinct microscopic pat-
terns (solid, micropapillary, lepidic, papillary or acinar) within the same 
tumor6,7. Lineage plasticity fundamentally confounds the intuition of 
human COO inference, as malignant cells may cease to resemble their

3067
Article
https://doi.org/10.1038/s41588-025-02418-5
Results
LUAD cells often adopt proximal lung identities
Applying standard approaches for scRNA-seq data processing and 
integration (see Methods), we compiled a lung cancer atlas of 225,931 
single-cell transcriptomes across lung cancer (21 LUAD; 12 LUSC; 
one large-cell neuroendocrine carcinoma(LCNEC); four NSCLC-not 
otherwise specified (NSCLC-NOS)) and non-cancer (18 benign 
lung samples and 21 adjacent normal) samples from 56 patients, 
including previously published data30–34 and ten samples from five 
patients with LUAD who were profiled as part of this study (Fig. 1a and 
Supplementary Table 1). Graph-based clustering of these data revealed 
40 distinct cell clusters, of which 20 harbored EPCAM gene expression, 
consistent with epithelial lineage (Extended Data Fig. 1a and Methods). 
Notably, among these 20 EPCAM+ clusters, we identified eight clusters 
of carcinoma cells (Extended Data Fig. 1b,c).
Next, to identify which benign lung cell types the carcinoma cells 
in our lung cancer atlas most closely resembled, we leveraged the 
Human Lung Cell Atlas (HLCA), a publicly available scRNA-seq atlas 
derived from non-cancerous lung tissue24 (see Methods) compris-
ing 584,444 (including 282,065 epithelial) cells. Notably, the HLCA 
classifies lung epithelial cells according to a multi-level taxonomy, 
the most granular comprising 23 categories, including rare (for example, 
ionocytes, tuft and neuroendocrine cell types) and recently charac­
terized cell types (for example, AT0, pre-TB secretory cells) (Fig. 1b 
and Extended Data Fig. 2).
From tumor scRNA-seq, we identified carcinoma cells on the 
basis of patient-specific aneuploidies (Extended Data Fig. 1b,c and 
presumed COO1. Although GEMMs serve as the de facto gold standard 
to validate cancer COOs, they only demonstrate sufficiency in specific 
driver and cell type contexts (for example, Kras mutations in AT2 cells)3. 
Hence, GEMM-based COO mappings may not be relevant to all human 
lung cancers, including those harboring complex driver co-mutations3. 
These challenges motivate the development of approaches to 
rigorously identify COOs in human lung cancer samples.
Most somatic mutations in cancer are passengers, with the 
majority arising before oncogenic transformation8. Studies over 
the past decade have linked genome-wide somatic mutation 
density to benign cell and tissue epigenetic states, including 
transcription9–11, replication timing12–16, histone marks17 and chromatin 
accessibility18–20. As these mutational imprints are fixed and not read-
ily erased by lineage plasticity, they may provide a more stable basis 
for COO inference than morphology or tumor cell transcriptomes. 
Previous studies have exploited this principle to derive classifiers 
that predict tissue-of-origin from the genome-wide distribution of 
passenger mutations18,21–23.
Single-cell RNA sequencing (scRNA-seq) has enabled unbiased 
profiling of cell states in complex human tissues, including the char-
acterization of new lung cell types24. However, these revised cellular 
taxonomies have not been used to investigate lung cancer COO, particu-
larly through the lens of passenger somatic mutations. To better under-
stand the relationship of cellular origin and transcriptional identity 
in human LUADs, we sought to integrate an atlas of benign lung 
cells24 with a compendium of primary human lung cancer whole- 
genome sequencing (WGS)11,25–29.

https://doi.org/10.1038/s41588-025-02418-5
Methods). We then labeled carcinoma cells from the tumor samples 
and benign cells from the normal and adjacent normal samples accord­
ing to the HLCA taxonomy, confirming accuracy against a panel of 
lung-associated markers (Extended Data Fig. 3a, Supplementary Table 2 
and Methods). Visual inspection of the uniform manifold approxima-
tion and projection of carcinoma cells on the scRNA-seq data revealed 
that lung carcinoma cells were liminal to several major lung epithelial 
cell identities, including AT2, AT1, basal and rare cell types (Fig. 1b). 
In addition, cells from LUSC, LUAD and LCNEC clustered in distinct 
regions (Fig. 1b).
We next probed the transcriptional identity of carcinoma cells. 
To subtract the effect of aneuploidies on genome-wide single-cell 
transcription, we projected scRNA-seq data of carcinoma cells onto 
the manifold of diploid (benign, non-carcinoma) epithelial cells in the 
HLCA and transferred labels from the nearest cell types (see Methods). 
We restricted our analysis to cases in which more than 50 carcinoma 
cells could be confidently (label uncertainty score of <0.5) assigned 
an identity, yielding 12 LUADs, seven LUSCs and one LCNEC. We found 
that only one-third of profiled LUADs (four out of 12) harbored carci-
noma cells with predominantly distal lung epithelial identities (Fig. 1c). 
Instead, the majority of cases (eight out of 12 LUADs) comprised carci-
noma cells with multiple non-distal identities, including basal, secre-
tory, tuft and neuroendocrine cells (Fig. 1c).
In contrast to LUAD, LUSC and LCNEC tumors exhibited more 
monomorphic cell identities consistent with their presumed 
COO. Specifically, LUSC cells harbored exclusively basal-like cell 
identities across all seven analyzed cases. Similarly, LCNEC cells 
harbored predominantly neuroendocrine identities (Fig. 1c). These 
results were consistent with the known histological pleomorphism 
of LUAD6,7.

footprints enables inference of COO. a, Schematic of analyses to infer lung

type-specific gene expression (referred to here as relative risk (bars)) and 95%

Passenger single-nucleotide variant footprints of proximal 
origins in some LUADs
Given evidence of non-distal phenotypes and lineage plasticity among 
LUAD cells, we sought to understand whether some of these alter-
nate identities reflected non-distal (non-alveolar) lung cell origins. 
To address this question, we considered an orthogonal source of 
evidence to investigate LUAD cellular origins. Transcription-coupled 
repair, a sub-pathway of nucleotide excision repair, preferen-
tially corrects DNA damage in highly expressed genes, causing an 
anti-correlation between gene expression and mutation density12,20. 
Other mechanisms, including increased access to DNA repair factors 
(for example, base excision repair), may also reduce mutation rates in 
highly expressed genes35.
Given that at least half of somatic mutations detected in NSCLC 
are thought to occur before the onset of tumorigenesis8, genes 
that are highly transcribed in their COO should have fewer somatic 
single-nucleotide variants (SNVs). To test this hypothesis, we ana­
lyzed mutation profiles from 242 LUAD and 53 LUSC whole genomes 
and observed gradually decreasing mutational density in LUAD and 
LUSC tumors in genes highly expressed in distal (AT2) and proximal 
(basal-resting) cell types, respectively (Extended Data Fig. 3b and 
Methods). In addition to considering all SNVs, we also analyzed the

patterns uncovers non-distal origins. a, Hierarchically clustered heatmap

gene-specific burden from individual signatures (see Methods). As 
has been previously described12, certain SNV mutational processes, 
such as tobacco (SBS4, SBS92) and aging (SBS1, SBS5), showed 
strong anti-correlation with average lung epithelial gene expression, 
while APOBEC (SBS2, SBS13) mutational density was uncorrelated 
(Extended Data Fig. 3c). Hence, to increase signal, we limited our regres­
sion analysis to SNVs arising from non-APOBEC mutational processes.
We next applied this approach to assess the association between 
expression and somatic mutation density in LUAD and LUSC, respec-
tively, across each of the 23 benign epithelial lung cell-type-specific 
gene expression centroids from the HLCA24 (see Methods). Our simula-
tions (Extended Data Fig. 3d,e) indicated that we would have substantial 
power and specificity to identify the correct COO across these cohorts 
by regressing individual cell types and choosing the cell type(s) with 
the strongest anti-correlation as the COO(s). Specifically, we quanti-
fied the strength of correlation between gene expression and mutation 
density as the log(relative risk) in a negative binomial regression, after 
correcting for standard mutational covariates, where log(relative 
risk) < 0 represents anti-correlation (Extended Data Fig. 3d,e, Supple-
mentary Note 1 and Methods). Notably, these simulations showed that 
for certain closely related cell types, such as subtypes of multiciliated 
cells, our approach was more effective at identifying the broader cell 
type category rather than the exact COO (Extended Data Fig. 3e, Sup-
plementary Note 1 and Methods).
As predicted, mutation density in the LUAD cohort was most 
strongly linked to cells of the distal lung; specifically, AT2 proliferat-
ing cells, identified recently through single-cell analyses (Fig. 2b). In 
LUSC, mutation density was most strongly anti-correlated with gene 
expression in two proximal lung epithelial cell types: suprabasal and 
hillock-like (Fig. 2c). These results confirmed that, in aggregate, LUAD 
and LUSC have distal and proximal cell origins, respectively.
Having confirmed AT2 origins in a cohort-level analysis of LUAD, 
we next investigated mutation density and normal cell gene expression 
correlations in individual LUAD samples. We were specifically inter-
ested in whether some LUADs showed mutational density footprints 
of cellular origins in non-distal lung cell types. We calculated a matrix, 
tabulating the strength of correlation (negative log(relative risk); see 
Methods) between the somatic SNV density profile for each LUAD 
sample and normal lung cell type pair. Clustering this matrix, we identi-
fied four groups (clusters 1–4; Fig. 3a and Methods) and calculated the 
COO for each group separately.
This analysis revealed that tumors in cluster 1 were associated 
with a distal COO; specifically, AT0 and AT2 proliferating cells (Fig. 3b). 
Cluster 2 showed weak associations with both proximal and distal 
cell types (Fig. 3b) and was thus classified as having an ambiguous 
COO. Finally, we found two clusters (clusters 3 and 4) comprising 
about 21% of LUAD samples that were consistent with a proximal COO, 
including suprabasal, hillock-like and basal-resting cells in cluster 3 and 
sub­mucosal glandular cells in cluster 4. A similar analysis applied to 
LUSC revealed that most tumors (87%) were associated with proximal 
cell types, although six LUSC tumors clustered in a group (cluster 1) 
with distal origins (AT0, AT2 proliferating) (Extended Data Fig. 4a,b).
Etiological and molecular differences in LUAD COO subtypes
Next, we wanted to understand how our COO designations corre-
lated with known etiological and molecular features of LUAD. Unlike 
LUSCs, LUADs occur in both smokers and never-smokers and are 
treated largely based on whether they harbor a targetable driver in 
one of several oncogenes (including KRAS, EGFR, ALK, ROS or RET). 
Annotating our cohort for known LUAD drivers (see Methods), we 
compared the prevalence of alterations across distal, ambiguous and 
proximal subgroups.
We first assessed smoking history and the exposures to key 
LUAD mutational processes across proximal, distal and ambigu­
ous origin LUADs. Notably, 58% of proximal origin LUADs were

self-reported never-smokers, significantly higher than the prevalence 
of never-smokers in distal (10%; P = 8.6 × 10−⁹, odds ratio (OR) = 12, 
Fisher’s Exact Test) or ambiguous origin LUADs (21%; P = 6.4 × 10−⁵, 
OR = 5.1) (Fig. 4a and Methods).
Consistent with this finding, distal origin LUADs harbored a high 
(≥75th percentile) burden of tobacco-related mutational signatures 
(SBS4, SBS92 and ID3) and total mutational burden (TMB) com-
pared to both proximal (SBS4 and SBS92: P = 2.6 × 10−8, OR = 20; ID3: 
P = 2.5 × 10−8, OR = 35; TMB: P = 1.2 × 10−5, OR = 9.9) and ambiguous 
origin (SBS4 and SBS92: P = 2.1 × 10−5, OR = 4.2; ID3: P = 5.1 × 10−3, 
OR = 2.5; TMB: P = 2.8 × 10−2, OR = 2.0) LUADs. Furthermore, ambig-
uous origin LUADs were more likely to be TMB-high compared to 
proximal origin LUADs (P = 9.6 × 10−3, OR = 4.8). Ambiguous origin 
LUADs were more frequently APOBEC-high (SBS2 and SBS13) than 
their distal (P = 4.4 × 10−4, OR = 3.3) or proximal (P = 7.8 × 10−6, OR = 8.7) 
origin counterparts (Fig. 4b, Supplementary Table 3 and Methods).
Several mutational signatures (SBS1, SBS5, ID1) have been previ-
ously associated with patient age at diagnosis, indicating the activity 
of distinct mutational processes occurring with each cell division 
or unit of time36. Interestingly, we found distinct distributions of 
aging-associated mutational signatures across LUAD COO groups. 
Proximal origin LUADs were more frequently SBS1-high (P = 6.0 × 10−3, 
OR = 3.2) and less frequently SBS5-high (P = 1.8 × 10−3, OR = 0.2) than 
distal origin LUADs. Ambiguous origin LUADs were more frequently 
ID1-high than distal (P = 8.6 × 10−3, OR = 2.5) or proximal (P = 3.9 × 10−2, 
OR = 2.3) origin LUADs (Fig. 4b and Supplementary Table 3). These 
results suggest that these distinct endogenous clock-like mutational 
processes may have cell-type-specific differences in their activity, 
which manifests in the somatic mutational profiles of the tumors 
that they give rise to.
Assessing significant (false discovery rate (FDR) < 0.25) associa­
tions between LUAD COO subgroups and driver status, we found that 
distal origin LUADs were enriched in KRAS driver mutations com-
pared to both proximal (P = 9.0 × 10−3, OR = 3.52) and ambiguous 
origin LUADs (P = 1.4 × 10−5, OR = 6.6). Distal origin LUADs showed 
higher rates of STK11 driver mutations (P = 4.0 × 10−3, OR = 3.5 vs. 
proximal origin; P = 2.9 × 10−3, OR = 2.8 vs. ambiguous origin) (Fig. 4c, 
Supplementary Table 3 and Methods). Proximal origin LUADs were 
enriched in EGFR mutations relative to distal origin LUADs (P = 5.6 × 10−3, 
OR = 5.5) and had fewer TP53 mutations than both distal (P = 2.0 × 10−3, 
OR = 0.34) or ambiguous origin LUADs (P = 8.4 × 10−4, OR = 0.30) 
(Fig. 4c, Supplementary Table 3 and Methods). Ambiguous origin 
LUADs had a higher prevalence of SMARCA4 and EGFR mutations 
than distal LUADs (SMARCA4: P = 6.2 × 10−4, OR = 7.1; EGFR: P = 1.2 × 10−2, 
OR = 4.2) (Fig. 4c and Supplementary Table 3). These results indicate 
that passenger mutational pattern-derived COO subgroups have 
biologically distinct evolutionary trajectories associated with 
distinct driver mutational combinations.
TP53 mutant LUADs have diminished distal identity
Having demonstrated that a subset of LUADs harbor non-distal 
transcriptional identities (Fig. 1c) and cell origins (Fig. 3a,b), we 
next sought to understand their interplay. To assess transcrip-
tional identity in bulk transcriptomes associated with WGS-profiled 
LUADs, we deconvolved the bulk RNA-seq data37 using a custom 
basis of six lung epithelial centroids from the broader lung cell type 
taxonomy (level 3; Extended Data Fig. 2) of the HLCA24 used to map 
lung carcinoma cell identities in scRNA-seq (Fig. 1c and Methods). 
We found that this approach had high accuracy (≥70%) for imputing 
predominant distal versus non-distal carcinoma cell identity across 
simu­lations (Supplementary Note 2) of bulk RNA-seq in high-purity 
(≥50%) tumors.
Applying this analysis to 75 LUAD tumors with at least 50% purity, 
we identified a substantial fraction of LUADs (55%, 34 out of 75) with a 
non-distal transcriptional identity (Fig. 5a), consistent with single-cell 
analyses (Fig. 1c). Notably, distal, proximal and ambiguous COO 
subgroups did not differ significantly in their rates of distal identity 
(Fig. 5a). Correlation of LUAD driver status with imputed cell iden-
tity revealed TP53 mutations as the only driver mutation enriched 
in non-distal versus distal identity LUADs (P = 1.1 × 10−7, OR = 21). 
Furthermore, non-distal (proximal or ambiguous) origin and TP53 
mutant LUADs were significantly less likely to have distal identity than 
their TP53 wild-type counterparts (P = 2.6 × 10−7, OR = 0.025) or TP53 
wild-type distal origin LUADs (P = 4.2 × 10−6, OR = 0.0) and TP53 mutant 
distal origin LUADs (P = 6.6 × 10−3, OR = 0.13) (Fig. 5b). Although the 
effects were most significant when grouping non-distal (proximal and 
ambiguous) origin LUADs, a concordant effect was seen when analyzing 
proximal and ambiguous origin LUADs separately (Fig. 5b)
These results suggest a complex interaction between TP53 
function and lineage plasticity in LUAD. Indeed, 86% (19 out of 22) 
of non-distal origin LUADs with wild-type TP53 had distal identity, 
consistent with non-distal-to-distal lineage plasticity, in contrast

to 12% (3 out of 25) of their TP53 mutant counterparts (P = 2.6 × 10−7, 
OR = 40; Extended Data Fig. 5a). In comparison, 47% of distal origin 
LUADs with TP53 mutations showed non-distal identity, consistent with

their wild-type counterparts (0%, 0 out of 9; P = 2.6 × 10−2, OR = Inf). 
Collectively, these results suggest that TP53 may promote or maintain 
distal identity during LUAD evolution, which can be compromised by 
non-distal origin and/or TP53 loss-of-function.

identity could be discerned through histology. To this end, we per-

(H&E) slides according to the latest (2021) WHO classification7, includ-
ing solid, papillary, micropapillary, lepidic, acinar, invasive mucinous, 
adenocarcinoma and cribriform subtypes (Extended Data Fig. 5b). A 
subset of tumors that could not be definitively diagnosed as LUAD 
on H&E re-review (immunohistochemistry is not available for TCGA 
tumors) were labeled as NSCLC-NOS. Although histology did not sig-
nificantly correlate with inferred LUAD origins, we found significant 
correlations between histology and identity (Extended Data Fig. 5c,d). 
These results were consistent with the notion that cancer cell identity, 
whether manifested through transcriptional states or histological 
appearance, may obscure the true cancer COO1.
Notably, most LUADs labeled as NSCLC-NOS had a non-distal 
identity (16 out of 19, 84%) that was significantly higher than the rate 
of non-distal identity in other LUAD histologies (16 out of 34, 47%; 
P = 1.1 × 10−4, OR = 10.9). Notably, all (19 out of 19, 100%) NSCLC-NOS 
histology tumors harbored a TP53 mutation, significantly more than 
other LUAD histologies (19 out of 44, 43%; P = 5.8 × 10−6, OR = Inf) and 
comprising over 40% of TP53 mutant LUADs (Extended Data Fig. 5e,f). 
In the absence of immunohistochemistry, we next assessed whether 
these non-distal-identity NSCLC-NOS tumors showed other molecu-
lar features consistent with LUADs. We found that 12 of 19 of these 
tumors harbored at least one classic LUAD-specific driver altera-
tion (for example, ALK fusion, EGFR mutation, SMARCA4, STK11 and 
KEAP1 mutations and NKX2-1 amplification; Extended Data Fig. 5g). 
Of the remaining seven, four harbored primarily LUSC-associated38 
drivers (FGFR1 and BCL2L1 amplifications), raising the possibility 
that they were misdiagnosed during their initial evaluation.
Lineage plasticity in an ambiguous origin LUAD
To further investigate the interplay of origin, identity and histology 
in LUAD, we used WGS to profile Weill Cornell Medicine (WCM)-1, 
a pre-treatment LUAD in our lung cancer atlas with predominantly 
non-distal identity (69% of carcinoma cells, predominantly neuroendo-
crine) (Fig. 6a). Correlating this sample’s SNV density profile with our panel 
of 23 benign lung epithelial gene expression centroids showed this case 
to be most similar to the ambiguous origin LUADs, with pre-TB secretory 
cells (proximal) and AT0 (distal) cells as the most strongly anti-correlated 
cell types (Fig. 3a, Fig. 6b and Extended Data Fig. 6a). Global analysis of 
single-cell transcriptomes from WCM-1 revealed two major carcinoma 
cell populations: WCM-1-A (n = 618, 32%) and WCM-1-B (n = 1297, 68%) 
(Extended Data Fig. 6b,c). These were associated with genetically dis-
tinct aneuploidies: WCM-1-A with loss of 15q and WCM-1-B with a loss of 
3p in addition to other copy number alterations (Fig. 6c). Although 
WCM-1-A cells had predominantly distal identity (85%, the majority 
of which were AT2), WCM-1-B cells showed predominantly non-distal 
identity (88.5%, the majority of which were neuroendocrine) (Fig. 6d).
Comparison of transcriptomes between these subpopulations 
uncovered high expression of PGC and CALCA in WCM-1-A and WCM-1-B 
cells, respectively (Fig. 6e). PGC encodes progastricin, a well-known 
marker of AT2 lineage, and CALCA encodes the neuroendocrine 
marker calcitonin related polypeptide alpha, also known as CGRP. 
Immunohistochemical staining of the WCM-1 tumor confirmed dif-
ferential marker protein expression across two geographically local-
ized and mutually exclusive PGC+ and CGRP+ cancer cell populations 
(Fig. 6f). Although genetically and immunohistochemically distinct, 
these cell populations were indistinguishable on H&E. These find-
ings demonstrate how the interplay of genome evolution and lineage 
plasticity may drive the evolution of cryptic non-distal identities in a 
pre-treatment LUAD, including towards a potentially therapeutically 
relevant neuroendocrine phenotype4,5.
Discussion
Defining the normal cell types that give rise to cancer has been challeng­
ing, particularly for human tumors in which lineage plasticity obscures 
cellular origins. By integrating passenger mutational landscapes 
from WGS with transcriptional states of benign lung epithelial cells, 
we establish a scalable framework for COO inference directly from 
patient tumors.
Our human-based COO analyses complement mouse model 
observations of LUADs arising in various allelic and cell type con-
texts, including from AT2 cells harboring activated Kras alleles and 
non-AT2 cells with alternative oncogenes (for example, Eml4–Alk)3. 
They also provide specific hypotheses about driver alteration–line-
age interactions that can be explored experimentally. This includes 
genomic evidence for LUADs of proximal origin, particularly in supra-
basal, basal-resting and submucosal glandular cells. These tumors 
are enriched in never-smokers and show reduced distal identity when 
TP53 is mutated, highlighting an unexpected interaction between 
COO, mutational context and transcriptional fate. Within distal ori-
gin tumors, we nominate AT2 proliferating and AT0 intermediates as 
candidate COOs. Both cellular populations expand in the setting of 
chronic lung injury, implicating dysregulated alveolar repair as a fertile 
ground for LUAD initiation39. Studying these tumors may illuminate 
the role of specific environmental insults, including air pollution, in 
lung cancer promotion40.
Our findings reposition lineage plasticity as an early, rather than 
late, feature of LUAD evolution. Although it has been previously stud-
ied in the context of relapse and therapeutic resistance, including 
recent elucidation of mechanisms of resistance to targeted KRAS 
inhibition41, distal-to-non-distal plasticity may be common in resect-
able pre-treatment LUADs, as our data suggests. The enrichment of 
SMARCA4 alterations and APOBEC3A activity among ambiguous COO 
tumors suggests potential interplay between molecular pathways 
driving lung cell dedifferentiation42,43 and APOBEC3 triggers, such as 
viral infection44.
Although our study is focused on lung cancer, correlations 
between passenger mutational patterns and normal cell transcription 
may enable COO inference in other tumor types. Extending our frame-
work to incorporate multi-omic atlases (for example, scRNA-seq and 
single-cell assay for transposase-accessible chromatin with sequenc-
ing) alongside WGS could further sharpen COO resolution and yield 
biologically meaningful subclassifications.
An important question is whether COO classifications have 
clinical relevance. At present, COO influences management only 
indirectly: lung cancers that are ineligible for targeted therapy are 
assigned distinct chemotherapy regimens on the basis of histology 
(pemetrexed-based for LUAD; gemcitabine-based or taxane-based 
for LUSC)45. Future translational studies correlating passenger 
mutation-based COO designations to treatment response will be essen-
tial to determine whether COO can serve as an actionable biomarker 
once clinical WGS becomes routine.

===== Stewart2026_NatGenet_CRISPR-OffTargets =====

Nature Genetics | Volume 58 | January 2026 | 20–27
20
nature genetics
Perspective
https://doi.org/10.1038/s41588-025-02428-3
Measurement and clinical interpretation of 
CRISPR off-targets
 
Ariella Angelini Stewart 
  1, Rebecca C. Ahrens-Nicklas2,3, Shengdar Q. Tsai 
  4, 
Kiran Musunuru 
  2,3, Petros Giannikopoulos 
  5 & Claire D. Clelland 
  1,5,6 
CRISPR genetic therapies are revolutionizing the landscape of preclinical 
research and clinical studies, providing new potential routes for curative 
intervention for a range of previously untreatable diseases. As with any 
therapy, the therapeutic benefits and risks must be weighed against 
consideration of the disease threat. Genome-related adverse events are an 
inherent risk of CRISPR genetic therapies, including off-target edits.  
The perception that CRISPR therapies ought to have near-zero off-targets 
belies clinical medicine, therapy development and biology, which 
demonstrate that ‘perfect’ therapeutics do not exist. Given that not all 
genomic off-target events are equal, we provide a practical framework to 
evaluate and assess off-target safety based on the tools available today 
and ones that will be developed in the future. With the comprehensive 
information and assessment gathered using these guidelines, we aim to 
streamline the transition of CRISPR therapeutics from bench to bedside.
With any new therapy, there is appropriate concern for potential 
adverse effects. CRISPR and other genetic therapies raise the possibility 
of genome-related adverse events. As these therapeutics evolve, there is 
also a need to develop tools that can predict and detect these off-target 
events. Researchers, clinicians developing CRISPR therapies and the 
regulatory bodies overseeing these new therapies aim for safe and 
effective therapies. Despite efforts to develop off-target-free CRISPR 
therapies, developers and regulators are likely to be faced with making 
clinically relevant decisions about both predictable and unpredict-
able unintended genomic changes resulting from CRISPR therapies. 
Here, we aim to dispel the notion that CRISPR must be perfect to be 
clinically viable and to create a clinically relevant framework for evalu-
ating off-targets to facilitate the creation of lifesaving therapies. We 
employ lessons from other therapeutic modalities and current clinical 
practice to examine CRISPR genome-related adverse event profiling 
in the broader context of responsible medical therapy development.
Since the landmark discovery by Doudna, Charpentier and col-
leagues in 2012 (ref. 1), CRISPR has been transforming the thera-
peutic landscape, particularly for previously ‘untreatable’ and fatal 
genetic diseases2,3. CRISPR gene therapies are used clinically for 
ex vivo editing4–7, with the first landmark Food and Drug Administra-
tion (FDA) approval of a CRISPR therapy5 for sickle cell disease in 2023 
and for β-thalassemia in 2024. More recently, in vivo genetic therapies 
are being tested in clinical trials8–15. For instance, a customized CRISPR 
treatment was designed, tested and administered to a neonate with a 
rare genetic disease within a critical time window before fatal or perma-
nent brain damage14, a milestone achievement for precision medicine. 
Precise genetic edits are achieved using a guide RNA that homes to a 
specific target DNA sequence within the genome, allowing its cognate 
Cas enzyme to create desired genomic changes, such as cuts, base edits 
or gene rewriting. It is now increasingly feasible to target nearly any 
genomic loci and thus design novel therapies that remove or correct 
disease-causing defects. As the Cas enzyme scans DNA for the perfect 
match to its guide RNA, it can also create undesired genomic off-target 
changes. Off-target edits typically occur at sites that share sequence 
similarity with the target region, but here we use the term to apply to 
any unintended genomic change, including alterations that begin at 
the on-target site, such as structural variant changes and chromosomal

This is because the cost of doing nothing (certain death) is part of the 
calculus. For example, the overwhelming majority of cancer treat-
ments risk secondary cancers due to DNA damage or immune disrup-
tion, which is even as high as 20% in some cases, but provide a chance 
for curative intervention21. Indeed, approximately 4–16% of patients 
receiving newer chimeric antigen receptor T cell therapies, which 
engineer blood stem cells to target cancer cells, develop a secondary 
cancer22. Nonetheless, the FDA has recently approved seven T cell gene 
therapies22. Gene replacement delivery vectors can also incur toxic 
side effects in addition to secondary cancer risks, such as damage to 
healthy tissues and cellular dysfunction from immune response24. 
Lentiviral gene therapies for adrenoleukodystrophy improve bleak 
survival rates but risk myelodysplasia23. When chemotherapy, radia-
tion and antiviral medications were first tested in humans, they were 
often poorly tolerated and toxic. This raises the question of whether 
radiation or chemotherapy, if discovered in the current regulatory and 
scientific climate, might have been deemed too toxic to try? The history 
of experimental medicine necessarily progresses with brave patients 
and clinicians willing to incur risk to push the boundaries of medicine. 
The appropriate patient population to trial new CRISPR therapeutics 
is those for whom the other side of the scale is weighted by death from 
genetic disease and who understand the known and unknown risks of 
CRISPR. Even with one or more known genomic off-targets or the risk 
of secondary cancer, a potentially curative CRISPR therapeutic may 
be preferred by patients over death.
In our collective clinical experience, we have noticed less risk toler-
ance in treating late-onset genetic diseases, even if fatal, compared to 
diseases that manifest from birth. For example, fatal genetic diseases 
that manifest in late childhood, adulthood or late in life are often met 
with less aggressive interventions and more fatalism than aggressive 
diseases apparent from birth. Why does being ‘born sick’ with a fatal or 
debilitating genetic condition tilt the risk calculus toward intervention 
compared to acquiring a fatal diagnosis in a ‘born healthy’ patient? 
We advocate for a paradigm shift in the way we consider patients with 
severe inherited genetic disorders, starting with a reexamination of 
the benefit–risk calculus for the development of new genetic therapies 
targeting diseases across the lifespan. We recommend advancing those 
damage. After a DNA change is induced by CRISPR, the DNA repair pro-
cess is dictated by the edited cell and varies depending on the cell type2, 
which can introduce additional intended or unwanted changes at the 
editing site. CRISPR base editors can also incur guide RNA-independent 
off-target editing events via their deaminase domains16,17.
A standard practice when developing and examining novel thera-
pies is to define the potential risks and weigh them against the potential 
benefits18,19. Due to the nature of gene editing, unintended damage 
to the genome is a potential side effect, such as chromosomal trans-
locations and truncations, aneuploidy, chromothripsis, rare loss of 
heterozygosity and small or large inversions and deletions20 (Fig. 1). 
The main concerns regarding off-target genomic changes are that 
a single ill-placed off-target change could result in cancer or that an 
accumulation of genomic off-targets could result in genotoxicity2,16,17,20. 
These secondary cancer and genotoxicity side effects are not unique to 
CRISPR-based genetic therapies but are present for other nongenetic 
standard-of-care therapies, such as chemotherapy21, radiation21 and 
gene replacement therapies22,23. We do not believe that CRISPR genetic 
therapies should be held to different benefit–risk ratio considera-
tions throughout the therapeutic development pipeline than these 
previously developed lifesaving therapeutics, which were at one time 
experimental. Indeed, delaying potentially lifesaving therapies in an 
effort to achieve perfection results in patient suffering and death.
Here, we discuss lessons from the development of other thera-
peutics, such as available off-target detection methods and the stand-
ard practice of patients and their physicians in weighing treatment 
risks and benefits to guide how we approach, measure and discuss 
genomic off-targets.
Calculating risk tolerance in relation to  
disease threat
High risk tolerance for therapies addressing life- 
threatening diseases
Patients and physicians routinely calibrate their willingness to assume 
risk of new or experimental therapies to the threat posed by disease. 
When faced with a life-threatening disease, the risk tolerance of patients 
and physicians often shifts to include therapies with inherent risks18,19.

Fig. 1 | Genomic on-target and off-target alterations that can occur after 
CRISPR editing. CRISPR-induced genomic off-targets may lead to adverse 
events including secondary cancer, cellular dysfunction or cell death. Through 
preclinical testing, the risk of each potential unintended genomic alteration is 
identified and evaluated. Some off-targets may be well tolerated, such as those 
occurring in genomic regions with no functional impact on coding or regulatory 
elements, while others could be deleterious, such as cancer-promoting 
mutations, large structural variants or multiple simultaneous off-target edits 
leading to cell death. Each off-target must be characterized by type and evaluated 
for risk to cellular and patient health to appropriately weigh off-target risk 
against potential benefit of the therapy.

https://doi.org/10.1038/s41588-025-02428-3
CRISPR therapies to clinic with safety profiles that outweigh the risk of 
fatal genetic disease, even if that profile includes some risk that both 
patient and physician are willing to incur.
Mid-to-high risk tolerance for therapies targeting  
life-altering diseases
The benefit–risk calculus is more measured for therapies that are not 
lifesaving but can instead modify high disease burden. For many dec-
ades, various oncologic treatments fell into this category by increas-
ing life expectancy by months or years or offering palliation without 
eradicating disease. It is possible that emerging CRISPR therapeutics 
may also tread the path from palliation to remission to cure as CRISPR 
technology and diagnostic and clinical trial landscapes for genetic 
diseases evolve together. The benefit–risk ratio in those cases, in which 
the outcome is ultimately not curative but involves extending or pre-
serving quality of life, is weighed against the substantially impaired 
quality of life. For CRISPR, such a scenario may result, for example, 
from intervening when the disease has advanced beyond the ability 
of a genetic intervention to halt the progression, such as late-stage, 
metastatic cancer, fulminant dementia or modifying a physiologic 
pathway that ameliorates but does not cure the disease. In these cases, 
preserving function or extending life may be desired by patients. Alzhei-
mer’s disease is an apt example with high disease morbidity, mortality 
and no cure. Recently approved anti-amyloid antibody treatments 
have become the first disease-modifying treatments available. Clini-
cal effects of anti-amyloid therapies are modest with a mild slowing in 
disease progression25,26. As many as one in three patients experience 
adverse brain bleeding or swelling25,26. Despite the high rate of adverse 
events, anti-amyloid therapy is offered to patients even without curative 
intent. We hold CRISPR therapeutics that have palliative rather than 
curative intent to a higher safety standard than CRISPR therapeutics 
targeting imminently deadly diseases with curative intent. Therapeu-
tics that modify disease without curing them should tilt toward safer 
benefit–risk ratios.
Given that the risks of new therapies are not fully understood 
until after controlled clinical trials have been performed and their use 
by broader patient populations after approval, we recommend that 
novel CRISPR therapies be first tested in patients for whom the risk of 
disease threat outweighs the risk of unknown adverse events. Thus, 
until CRISPR genetic therapy safety profiles are well established, we 
recommend testing in patients with life-threatening or life-altering 
diseases before proceeding to moderate or low disease threat. When 
the cost of doing nothing is certain death, patients and their physicians 
are often willing to assume greater risk, an ethos that continues into 
the modern age of precision medicine.
Recommendations on testing and interpreting 
CRISPR-induced genomic off-targets
What data are needed to inform patients, clinicians and regulatory 
bodies of the genomic safety of a potential new CRISPR therapeutics? 
The US FDA and the European Medicines Agency provide general rec-
ommendations for monitoring genotoxicity in preclinical and clini-
cal studies27,28, recommending that researchers optimize the genetic 
engineering system and delivery method ‘to reduce the potential for 
off-target genome modification, to the extent possible’ (ref. 28). Design 
factors that can change on-target and off-target rates, such as delivery 
components, CRISPR reagents and the time window of editing, are an 
integral part of this analysis (Fig. 2).
To detect and profile off-targets, the FDA recommends using 
‘multiple methods (e.g., in silico, biochemical, cellular-based assays) 
that include a genome-wide analysis…to reduce bias in identifica-
tion of potential off-target sites’ (ref. 28). A wide range of possible 
tools and methods to identify off-targets are available. Others have 
covered off-target detection methods in detail16,17,20,29–31; here, we aim 
to briefly summarize current practices in the field for techniques 
enabling investigational new drugs. The general approach of many 
preclinical studies is to first nominate the most likely off-targets 
using a combination of in silico and empirical methods and to then 
verify which of the nominated events are actually present, and, if so, 
at what frequency, in a relevant in vitro human cell line model or in 
primary human cells that have been exposed to the editor in question 
in representative conditions.
Off-target nomination using in silico and experimental methods
In silico methods (Table 1) are used to computationally predict poten-
tial off-target sites based on guide RNA sequence similarity, typically 
measured against a reference genome. This strategy is most applica-
ble to highly conserved genomic regions but may fail for on-target 
and off-target genomic sites with variation among humans32. While 
patients may have meaningful differences not captured in the refer-
ence genome, sequencing each patient in the development phase 
of a therapy intended for large patient populations is also currently 
impractical. This dichotomy was addressed during the development 
of the CRISPR genetic therapy, Casgevy, now approved by the FDA. The 
original in silico predicted off-targets were not observed in cell-based 
assays5, but additional off-targets were observed and validated using a 
variant-aware in silico off-target prediction tool called CRISPRme that 
can account for polymorphisms in human populations32 and in vitro 
cell lines with the single-nucleotide variant that differed from the 
reference genome.
Typically, guide RNAs are designed to have only one perfect 
match in the genome: the on-target site. But guide RNAs may have up 
to thousands of potential binding sites in the genome that tolerate 
a mismatch between the guide RNA and the off-target binding site. 
Alignment-based (Cas-OFFinder33), scoring (CRISPOR34), machine 
learning (Elevation35), deep learning (DeepCRISPR36) and other in 
silico methods are used to identify and rank potential off-target sites. 
However, it is challenging to analyze hundreds to thousands of com-
putationally predicted potential off-targets to find true off-targets, a 
process that requires empiric testing.

editing rates can be affected by the delivery method, the Cas system, guide

23
Perspective
https://doi.org/10.1038/s41588-025-02428-3
Accurate profiling of human targets must be performed using 
human samples. Animal models are not suitable for genomic off-target 
detection due to considerable sequence differences, particularly in 
noncoding regions, between humans and other species2. Naked DNA 
methods support off-target nomination by providing evidence that 
CRISPR can alter DNA at a predicted site with methods based on cut-
ting or alteration of naked DNA37–39, chromatin40 or circularized naked 
DNA41–43 (Table 1). However, naked DNA methods overestimate the 
number of true off-targets, sometimes by hundreds or thousands of 
sites44. This is because altering naked DNA is easier than altering DNA 
in a cell, and random damage of unprotected naked DNA can mimic 
an off-target. Apparent false positive discovery rates for naked DNA 
methods have been reported between 27% and 95%16,29, while cellular 
methods tend to have less than 20%16,29. Therefore, testing or validating 
off-targets in human cellular systems is critical.
Experimental testing to verify off-targets
After in silico and cell-free methods predict potential off-target sites, 
true on-target and off-target editing rates must be confirmed empiri-
cally (Table 1). Cell-based assays can provide empirical nomination and 
verification with additional information regarding rates of how often 
these changes occur, albeit in a typically simplified cellular system that 
may or may not recapitulate the performance of the editor in the human 
body. Cell-based methods45,46, such as GUIDE-seq42,47, which nominate 
off-target locations, are followed up with additional sequencing-based 
experiments48. To verify off-targets, nominated genomic regions can 
be amplified by PCR and sequenced to increase sequencing depth and 
thus detection rates of potential off-target regions. Genomic rearrange-
ments may be detected in cellulo49 or with methods independent of 
sequencing, such as optical genome mapping50. Modified detection 
methods for noncanonical CRISPR systems can detect RNA edits when 
performing base editing16,17, changes in chromatin structure for epige-
netic editing31 and tagging methods for prime editing51–53.
Cell-based assays are more difficult to optimize, more expensive 
and currently often use cell types (such as HEK or U2OS cancer cell lines) 
initially optimized for blood-related disorders (T cell editing)41 that may 
not represent other clinical settings. Testing in clinically relevant cell 
or tissue types may not yet be possible if the cells or tissues are difficult 
to grow in a laboratory or susceptible to the cytotoxicity of cell-based 
assays. Current methodologies may also prioritize off-targets that are 
favored by the repair mechanism of the tested cell, which may differ 
from the repair outcome of the target tissues in patients.
Ideally, unbiased whole-genome methodologies that have high 
depth (number of times a base pair is read) and coverage (percentage of 
the genome that is read) should be used to survey the entire sequenced 
genome for off-targets. In practice, the major limitation to adopting 
genome-wide surveys is the cost of sequencing and the bioinformatic 
tools to quantify changes on a genome-wide scale. However, as the 
cost of sequencing continues to decrease and the depth and coverage 
of sequencing increase, it is likely that there will be a move to unbiased 
whole-genome off-target sequencing methodologies in the future. 
As our ability to accurately characterize editing outcomes continues 
to increase through technology and assay advancements, it will be 
possible to further understand the implications of unintended editing 
outcomes, such as loss of heterozygosity, large deletions or structural 
rearrangements, on cellular function. Unbiased detection methods 
including whole-genome sequencing also have the additional advan-
tage of capturing unpredictable guide RNA-independent off-targets 
that are not related to the guide RNA sequence, such as edits that might 
result from guide RNA contamination during manufacturing.
In addition to guidance on somatic off-target risk, the FDA has 
taken a special interest in germline editing. A clinical hold was placed 
on Verve Therapeutics’ pioneering in vivo PCSK9-targeted base-editing 
trial10 for the treatment of familial hypercholesterolemia when the FDA 
requested quantification of germline editing. In this trial and in similar 
trailblazing preclinical studies by Intellia Therapeutics for hereditary 
angioedema12 and transthyretin amyloidosis9, no editing was detected 
in treated male nonhuman primate sperm9,10,12. Although editing was 
observed in bulk ovarian tissue9,12, it was not observed in progeny from 
gene-edited female mice10,12, confirming a lack of germline transmission 
of the introduced edits. Lack of germline editing more likely reflects 
the inability of the drug delivery vehicles, lipid nanoparticles in these 
cases, to penetrate dense tissue, rather than innate protection from 
germline editing. As delivery vectors become more potent, the issue 
of germline editing will remain a concern, and therefore there is as yet 
no clear guidance on how potential germline editing will impact regu-
latory approval of future therapies. Germline heritability of CRISPR 
edits is a sensitive issue given our incomplete understanding of many 
genomic changes across lifespan and because offspring of the treated 
individual may incur the risk of an off-target without consenting to 
the therapy. In cases of novel CRISPR treatments that pose a risk of 
germline editing, it is our opinion that lifesaving treatments for oth-
erwise untreatable diseases should not be withheld or substantially 
delayed due to a potential risk of germline editing. Instead, we should 
employ clinical measures to mitigate the impact of germline mutagen-
esis, as routinely employed in patients of childbearing age receiving 
chemotherapy, radiation and teratogenic medications18,19,21. Clinical 
mitigation measures include appropriate counseling, options for 
fertility preservation (such as oocyte, sperm and embryo banking), 
reproductive planning and appropriate birth control. In contrast to 
life-threatening diseases, for which we recommend that the risk of ger-
mline editing not unduly delay or prevent a lifesaving CRISPR therapy, 
for low-risk and non-life-threatening diseases, it may be appropriate 
to demonstrate minimal germline editing risk, even if clearing this 
hurdle delays the therapy. Furthermore, learning about the potential 
for germline editing in human patients who have already been treated 
with in vivo CRISPR therapies would be beneficial. This can readily be 
achieved by measuring editing events in sperm and donated oocytes 
from patients who have participated in clinical trials. Such monitoring 
may become routine as CRISPR editing reagents and delivery vehicles 
become more potent, increasing the chance that a future editor could 
penetrate gametes.

Nature Genetics | Volume 58 | January 2026 | 20–27
24
Perspective
https://doi.org/10.1038/s41588-025-02428-3
Recommendations for evaluating the risks of 
predicted or known off-targets
As off-target profiling methods continue to advance, we will be able to 
detect low-frequency or rare off-target events with increased efficiency. 
Achieving CRISPR-based genetic therapies with zero off-targets may 
not be possible for many viable therapeutic candidates owing to the 
tolerance of the Cas enzyme for mismatches between guide RNA and 
target DNA. In Table 2, we offer a practical framework rooted in the 
available sequencing technologies for detecting, interpreting and 
prioritizing genomic off-targets for emerging CRISPR therapeutics.
Ideally, effective on-target guide RNAs are desired that do not 
cause cancer, do not disrupt untargeted protein function and do not 
give rise to long-term adverse events. In accordance with the FDA28, 
we recommend unbiased sequencing methods that are appropriate 
for the target cell or tissue to detect off-targets in genomic loci associ-
ated with cancer risk or loci that may disrupt cellular function. While 
detection and interpretation of off-targets is rapidly evolving given 
advancements in sequencing and other technologies and discoveries 
in the biological understanding of human genomes, the principles we 
outline here to select appropriate tools for off-target detection and 
the framework to interpret findings are applicable to both current 
and future tools.
Most experimental assays to characterize off-target effects cur-
rently use supersaturating doses of the editor, which overestimate 
off-target rates, and cells that may not use the same type of DNA repair 
system as the intended tissues. We recommend that on-target and 
off-target edit outcome profiling is performed in the relevant cell type 
that uses the matching predicted in vivo DNA repair system when pos-
sible and at physiological doses to replicate the most physiologically 
likely off-target outcomes. Quantification of small DNA changes 
and large structural changes at both the on-target site and possible 
off-target sites using cells with the same DNA repair system as the 
tissues receiving the final product should be included in this analysis.
One of the most important considerations is to limit the risk of 
secondary cancer. We recommend that careful attention is paid to 
editing of known oncogenes and tumor-suppressor genes, which could 
increase the risk of secondary cancer. Such editing can occur from 
the editor itself or from the delivery vehicle such as AAV integration 
at on-target or off-target cut sites54,55. By referencing the Catalogue of 
Somatic Mutations56 and deep sequencing to lower the threshold of 
detection of rare but meaningful events, off-target cancer mutation 
detection may be standardized across platforms and targets. Over 
time, knowledge from standardized testing will increase our ability 
to infer whether specific genomic changes, such as to unmapped or 
poorly characterized regulatory elements, will increase the risk of 
oncogenesis. It is also recommended to perform some of these experi-
ments at supersaturating doses to increase detection of low-probability 
off-targets that could produce cancer-causing mutations. We recom-
mend the establishment of a field-wide intolerance for any known 
cancer-promoting edit at adequate, low thresholds of detection.
To evaluate the risk of the CRISPR therapy disrupting cellular 
function, we recommend profiling whether an off-target occurs in a 
site with known functional impact such as either a coding sequence 
or a regulatory noncoding region57,58. Avoiding approaches or guide 
RNAs that result in changes to coding sequences or known regulatory 
elements is prudent. To do this, one must know which organs and cell 
types will be edited and whether the genomic change alters cellular 
function in those tissues. It is important here to distinguish genomic
